English for Work · Customers & creative work

Baristas and Cafe Staff English

Clarify drink orders, explain coffee choices, check milk and syrup requests, coordinate pickup, discuss retail beans, and resolve cafe order problems.

8 practical lessonsIntermediate to advanced1 complete learner book
Start practicing →

Words into action

Find your words.
Make yourself clear.

For Baristas, cafe counter staff, and coffee pickup staff.

Open learner book (PDF) ↗

Copy complete AI prompts ↓

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Separate size, temperature, and coffee choice, then give a concise read-back before the drink is made.
  • Compare drinks on the requested feature while distinguishing texture, espresso serving, and unsupported caffeine claims.
  • Acknowledge an allergy, preserve the separate modification, and route the actual product and preparation questions for checking.
  • Interrupt a mistaken pickup politely, match the ticket and drink, and explain readiness without inventing a wait.
  • Identify an order-label mismatch, request a checked remake, and keep timing and payment decisions separate.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

All cafes, recipes, tickets, prices, and incidents are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not food-safety certification, medical advice, equipment operation, or cash-control procedures. Follow actual product information, local requirements, workplace allergen controls, equipment instructions, and payment rules. No example authorizes serving an unverified drink or bypassing a required check.

01Taking a precise drink orderWhat does regular mean?+

Your goal

Separate size, temperature, and coffee choice, then give a concise read-back before the drink is made.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

What does regular mean?

At this fictional cafe, regular is the middle cup size. Customer Alex asks for a regular latte, meaning non-decaf coffee rather than a particular size. Barista Nia has not started the drink. When asked separately, Alex chooses a small hot latte, non-decaf, with the standard dairy milk and no syrup, to take away. The task is to resolve the ambiguous word without correcting the customer as if there were one universal cafe vocabulary. No price, exact temperature, or caffeine quantity is supplied.

Nia: Barista / Alex: Customer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does regular mean on this cafe menu?

Answer and explanation

C. The middle size

The brief defines the local size convention, not a universal coffee meaning.

2. What did Alex initially mean?

Answer and explanation

D. Non-decaf coffee

Alex uses regular for coffee choice rather than cup size.

3. Which order is confirmed?

Answer and explanation

D. Small hot non-decaf latte, standard dairy milk, no syrup, takeaway

The separate answers establish every listed detail in the confirmed order.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Regular is not a reliable substitute for a complete order. In this cafe it names a size, but Alex means non-decaf. Ask which meaning is intended, then confirm the remaining choices separately.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
barista
Worker who prepares and serves coffee drinks.ask the barista
drink order
Specified beverage and selected variations.confirm the drink order
cup size
Named or measured capacity option for a drink.clarify the cup size
regular
Context-dependent label that may describe size or coffee choice.clarify regular
small
Cafe-defined size below its larger options.order a small latte
hot
Prepared as a hot drink rather than an iced version.confirm hot or iced
iced
Served with ice according to the drink recipe.make an iced latte
non-decaf
Coffee not sold as decaffeinated.specify non-decaf coffee
decaf
Coffee processed to remove most caffeine, not necessarily all.request decaf
latte
Espresso-based drink with milk; proportions depend on the recipe.order a latte
espresso
Concentrated coffee brewed by forcing hot water through ground coffee under pressure.prepare espresso
shot
Cafe serving of espresso, with size defined by its recipe.confirm the shot count
standard milk
Milk used by default in a particular recipe.identify the standard milk
dairy milk
Milk from an animal source rather than a plant-based drink.specify dairy milk
plant-based drink
Beverage made from plant ingredients and used as a milk alternative.check a plant-based drink
syrup
Liquid flavoring or sweetener added to a drink.omit the syrup
modifier
Order detail that changes the standard item.enter the modifier
takeaway
Order intended to be taken away from the premises.mark as takeaway
for here
Order intended for consumption on the premises.ask for here or to go
read-back
Spoken repetition used to confirm the received order.give a read-back
clarifying question
Question that resolves a specific uncertainty.ask a clarifying question
size convention
Local system for naming cup sizes.explain the size convention
order entry
Recording of the selected item and its details.check the order entry
preparation start
Point at which making the drink begins.confirm before preparation start

A shot is a recipe-defined serving, not a universal caffeine quantity. Keep small, hot, non-decaf, milk choice, and syrup choice separate. None of those words alone supplies the price or an exact caffeine amount.

C Notice the language

A clarification can sound welcoming

A short either-or question is often easier to answer than a list of technical terms. Explain your local size name without suggesting the customer used English incorrectly. Confirm choices in the same order each time so the read-back is easy to follow.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Resolve the first word
When you say regular, do you mean size or non-decaf?
Explain the local label
Here, regular is our middle size.
Ask for size
Would you like small, regular, or large?
Separate temperature
Would you like that hot or iced?
Confirm coffee choice
Non-decaf coffee, correct?
Ask about milk
Would you like our standard dairy milk?
Check syrup
Any syrup, or none?
Check destination
Is that for here or to take away?
Repeat the core order
One small hot latte with non-decaf coffee.
Keep the omission explicit
No syrup in that one.
Confirm the milk
Standard dairy milk for this order.
Invite a correction
Have I got all of that right?
Pause preparation
I will confirm the details before we make it.
Correct the size
Small, not our regular size.
Acknowledge the clarification
Thanks; I understand which meaning you intended.
Close the read-back
Small, hot, non-decaf, standard milk, no syrup, takeaway.
6 language and precision notes
Regular versus standard
Both depend on local context; name the feature each describes.
Separate questions
Ask size and temperature separately when the original order is ambiguous.
Non-decaf
This describes the coffee choice without inventing a caffeine quantity.
No syrup
An explicit omission is easier to preserve than an assumed default.
For here or to go
To go is a common US alternative to takeaway.
Read-back rhythm
Group the item first, then size, temperature, and modifications.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which question directly resolves the ambiguity?

Answer and explanation

C. Do you mean our middle size or non-decaf coffee?

The correct question names two plausible meanings without imposing either.

2. Which statement overclaims?

Answer and explanation

D. Non-decaf tells me the exact caffeine quantity.

Coffee choice alone does not establish an exact caffeine quantity.

3. Which correction preserves Alex's request?

Answer and explanation

B. Small, not regular size; still non-decaf.

The correction changes the size interpretation without changing the confirmed coffee choice.

4. Why read the order back?

Answer and explanation

A. To verify the combined choices before preparation

A read-back confirms the details together and lets the customer correct a misunderstanding.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

What does regular mean?

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Nia: Barista / Alex: Customer

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • dairy milk
  • regular
  • small
  • order entry
  • temperature choice
  • read-back
  • non-decaf
  • no syrup
  • takeaway
  • size convention
  1. Alex
    Could I have a regular latte to take away? I can see three sizes, but I am not sure which one to ask for.
  2. Nia
    Of course. By 1, do you mean our middle size or coffee that is not decaf?
  3. Alex
    Not decaf. Actually, I only want a small cup. I thought regular meant the normal coffee, not the size.
  4. Nia
    No problem. I will put the size through as 2. Would you like it hot or iced?
  5. Alex
    Hot, please. I do not need an extra shot or anything like that. Just the usual coffee in the small latte.
  6. Nia
    Understood: small, hot, 3. No extra shot.
  7. Alex
    What milk does it come with? I could not see that part of the menu from the queue.
  8. Nia
    The standard is 4. Is that what you would like?
  9. Alex
    Yes, that is fine. And no syrup, please. I do not want vanilla or another added flavor.
  10. Nia
    I have added 5. Are you drinking it here or taking it with you?
  11. Alex
    Taking it with me. Sorry, I think I said that at the start, but there is quite a bit of noise here.
  12. Nia
    You did. Thanks for confirming 6. I will keep that on the ticket.
  13. Alex
    Can I check one thing? If I say regular next time, will that mean the middle cup here?
  14. Nia
    Yes, that is our 7: small, regular, large. You can say small with non-decaf coffee to make both choices clear.
  15. Alex
    Right. Small latte, non-decaf. And still hot, even though we have changed what I first called the size?
  16. Nia
    Still hot. The 8 has not changed.
  17. Alex
    Thanks. Could you run through the whole order before you send it to the bar?
  18. Nia
    Here is the 9: small hot latte, non-decaf, standard dairy milk, no syrup, takeaway.
  19. Alex
    That is exactly right. Just the one drink, please.
  20. Nia
    Great. The 10 now matches that. We have not started the drink yet; the bar will use these details.

Answers and explanations
  1. regular Turn 2. Regular has two plausible meanings here; asking first prevents a wrong size or coffee choice.
  2. small Turn 4. Small is Alex's chosen size; it does not change the separate non-decaf request.
  3. non-decaf Turn 6. Non-decaf identifies the coffee choice, not an exact caffeine amount or an added espresso serving.
  4. dairy milk Turn 8. Dairy milk names the actual default category, which the customer needs before choosing.
  5. no syrup Turn 10. The explicit no-syrup instruction preserves the customer's request on the order.
  6. takeaway Turn 12. Takeaway repeats the service choice without changing the drink recipe.
  7. size convention Turn 14. Size convention refers to this cafe's small, regular, and large labels, not a universal meaning.
  8. temperature choice Turn 16. Temperature choice is independent of size and remains hot in the confirmed order.
  9. read-back Turn 18. A read-back combines all the separate answers so the customer can check the whole order before preparation.
  10. order entry Turn 20. Order entry is the confirmed recorded request, not a statement that the drink is already prepared.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Alex: Could I have a regular latte to take away? I can see three sizes, but I am not sure which one to ask for.
  2. Nia: Of course. By regular, do you mean our middle size or coffee that is not decaf?
  3. Alex: Not decaf. Actually, I only want a small cup. I thought regular meant the normal coffee, not the size.
  4. Nia: No problem. I will put the size through as small. Would you like it hot or iced?
  5. Alex: Hot, please. I do not need an extra shot or anything like that. Just the usual coffee in the small latte.
  6. Nia: Understood: small, hot, non-decaf. No extra shot.
  7. Alex: What milk does it come with? I could not see that part of the menu from the queue.
  8. Nia: The standard is dairy milk. Is that what you would like?
  9. Alex: Yes, that is fine. And no syrup, please. I do not want vanilla or another added flavor.
  10. Nia: I have added no syrup. Are you drinking it here or taking it with you?
  11. Alex: Taking it with me. Sorry, I think I said that at the start, but there is quite a bit of noise here.
  12. Nia: You did. Thanks for confirming takeaway. I will keep that on the ticket.
  13. Alex: Can I check one thing? If I say regular next time, will that mean the middle cup here?
  14. Nia: Yes, that is our size convention: small, regular, large. You can say small with non-decaf coffee to make both choices clear.
  15. Alex: Right. Small latte, non-decaf. And still hot, even though we have changed what I first called the size?
  16. Nia: Still hot. The temperature choice has not changed.
  17. Alex: Thanks. Could you run through the whole order before you send it to the bar?
  18. Nia: Here is the read-back: small hot latte, non-decaf, standard dairy milk, no syrup, takeaway.
  19. Alex: That is exactly right. Just the one drink, please.
  20. Nia: Great. The order entry now matches that. We have not started the drink yet; the bar will use these details.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Regular needs three checks

The ambiguous latte order from the case.

  1. Customer: Could I have a regular latte, please, and make that one to go?
  2. Barista: By regular, do you mean our middle size or the caffeinated coffee option?
  3. Customer: The middle size. I'd actually like decaf because I've already had coffee today.
  4. Barista: Certainly. Would you like that latte hot or iced on this order?
  5. Customer: Hot, please. I didn't realize regular could refer to more than the size.
  6. Barista: It can. Which milk would you like? We have whole milk and the listed alternatives.
  7. Customer: Whole milk is fine. I don't want any syrup added to the drink.
  8. Barista: That's one middle-size hot decaf latte, whole milk, no syrup, to take away.
  9. Customer: Yes, that's everything. Please use the name Ellis when you call the order.
  10. Barista: Ellis, thank you. I'll put that name with those specifications on your ticket.
A shot is not a bigger cup

A customer asks what an extra-shot option changes.

  1. Customer: Does choosing an extra shot make the drink come in a bigger cup?
  2. Barista: No, it adds espresso under our menu option; the cup size stays the same.
  3. Customer: I want a larger drink, not extra coffee in the size I already chose.
  4. Barista: Then let's change the size rather than automatically add an extra shot to it.
  5. Customer: Thank you. What's the price difference between the small and large sizes here?
  6. Barista: The large is one dollar more. An extra shot has a separate listed charge.
  7. Customer: I'll take the large standard version, then, without choosing that extra-shot option today.
  8. Barista: Understood. Large latte, standard coffee recipe, with no separately added extra shot.
  9. Customer: And still hot with whole milk, as I said when I first ordered.
  10. Barista: Yes, those choices stay unchanged. I'll repeat the final ticket before taking payment.
Leaving room is an amount request

A customer clarifies space in a filter-coffee cup.

  1. Barista: You asked me to leave room. How much space would you like for your milk?
  2. Customer: Just a little at the top, not a cup that's only half full.
  3. Barista: Thanks. Would you like the milk added here, or served separately for you?
  4. Customer: Separately, please. I'd like to decide how much to add at the table.
  5. Barista: I'll note room for milk and a separate serving, with no syrup added.
  6. Customer: Yes. This is for drinking here, so I don't need a takeaway lid.
  7. Barista: A dine-in cup, then. Is the coffee choice still the house filter?
  8. Customer: It is. I haven't changed the coffee itself, only how it's served today.
  9. Barista: Understood. House filter with room and milk separately, for here rather than takeaway.
  10. Customer: Exactly. Thank you for checking what I meant before reducing the amount too much.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

What does regular mean?
  1. Read Nia's questions and Alex's answers with the cloze words restored.
  2. Repeat the final order in this order: size, temperature, coffee choice, milk, syrup, destination.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Confirm a different ambiguous order

Customer Jo calls their usual order regular coffee, then explicitly clarifies that it must be decaf. After separate questions, Jo chooses a large iced latte with oat drink. Use these four confirmed details.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Barista: "The coffee choice is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. decaf

Jo explicitly clarifies decaf as the intended coffee choice.

2. Customer: "The size should be ___."

Answer and explanation

D. large

Large is the selected cup size in this new order.

3. Barista: "The drink should be ___."

Answer and explanation

A. iced

Iced is the stated temperature style, not inferred from size.

4. Customer: "Please use ___."

Answer and explanation

C. oat drink

Oat drink is the specified milk alternative for this order.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Extend the vocabularyPractice the grammarRehearse with AI

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Taking a precise drink order
Original fictional case: At this fictional cafe, regular is the middle cup size. Customer Alex asks for a regular latte, meaning non-decaf coffee rather than a particular size. Barista Nia has not started the drink. When asked separately, Alex chooses a small hot latte, non-decaf, with the standard dairy milk and no syrup, to take away. The task is to resolve the ambiguous word without correcting the customer as if there were one universal cafe vocabulary. No price, exact temperature, or caffeine quantity is supplied.
Language workshop: What does regular mean?
Communication goal: Separate size, temperature, and coffee choice, then give a concise read-back before the drink is made.
Language guidance: Regular is not a reliable substitute for a complete order. In this cafe it names a size, but Alex means non-decaf. Ask which meaning is intended, then confirm the remaining choices separately.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): When you say regular, do you mean size or non-decaf? / Here, regular is our middle size. / Would you like small, regular, or large? / Would you like that hot or iced? / Non-decaf coffee, correct? / Would you like our standard dairy milk? / Any syrup, or none? / Is that for here or to take away? / One small hot latte with non-decaf coffee. / No syrup in that one. / Standard dairy milk for this order. / Have I got all of that right? / I will confirm the details before we make it. / Small, not our regular size. / Thanks; I understand which meaning you intended. / Small, hot, non-decaf, standard milk, no syrup, takeaway.
Vocabulary: barista: Worker who prepares and serves coffee drinks. / drink order: Specified beverage and selected variations. / cup size: Named or measured capacity option for a drink. / regular: Context-dependent label that may describe size or coffee choice. / small: Cafe-defined size below its larger options. / hot: Prepared as a hot drink rather than an iced version. / iced: Served with ice according to the drink recipe. / non-decaf: Coffee not sold as decaffeinated. / decaf: Coffee processed to remove most caffeine, not necessarily all. / latte: Espresso-based drink with milk; proportions depend on the recipe. / espresso: Concentrated coffee brewed by forcing hot water through ground coffee under pressure. / shot: Cafe serving of espresso, with size defined by its recipe. / standard milk: Milk used by default in a particular recipe. / dairy milk: Milk from an animal source rather than a plant-based drink. / plant-based drink: Beverage made from plant ingredients and used as a milk alternative. / syrup: Liquid flavoring or sweetener added to a drink. / modifier: Order detail that changes the standard item. / takeaway: Order intended to be taken away from the premises. / for here: Order intended for consumption on the premises. / read-back: Spoken repetition used to confirm the received order. / clarifying question: Question that resolves a specific uncertainty. / size convention: Local system for naming cup sizes. / order entry: Recording of the selected item and its details. / preparation start: Point at which making the drink begins.
Speaking task: Read Nia's questions and Alex's answers with the cloze words restored. Repeat the final order in this order: size, temperature, coffee choice, milk, syrup, destination. Complete Jo's four-line order and check that decaf did not become a size.
Partner: Nia: Barista / Alex: Customer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Customer Jo calls their usual order regular coffee, then explicitly clarifies that it must be decaf. After separate questions, Jo chooses a large iced latte with oat drink. Use these four confirmed details.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Alex: Thanks. Could you run through the whole order before you send it to the bar?
Nia: Here is the read-back: small hot latte, non-decaf, standard dairy milk, no syrup, takeaway.
Alex: That is exactly right. Just the one drink, please.
Nia: Great. The order entry now matches that. We have not started the drink yet; the bar will use these details.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Language guidance: Regular is not a reliable substitute for a complete order. In this cafe it names a size, but Alex means non-decaf. Ask which meaning is intended, then confirm the remaining choices separately.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): When you say regular, do you mean size or non-decaf? / Here, regular is our middle size. / Would you like small, regular, or large? / Would you like that hot or iced? / Non-decaf coffee, correct? / Would you like our standard dairy milk? / Any syrup, or none? / Is that for here or to take away? / One small hot latte with non-decaf coffee. / No syrup in that one. / Standard dairy milk for this order. / Have I got all of that right? / I will confirm the details before we make it. / Small, not our regular size. / Thanks; I understand which meaning you intended. / Small, hot, non-decaf, standard milk, no syrup, takeaway.
Vocabulary: barista: Worker who prepares and serves coffee drinks. / drink order: Specified beverage and selected variations. / cup size: Named or measured capacity option for a drink. / regular: Context-dependent label that may describe size or coffee choice. / small: Cafe-defined size below its larger options. / hot: Prepared as a hot drink rather than an iced version. / iced: Served with ice according to the drink recipe. / non-decaf: Coffee not sold as decaffeinated. / decaf: Coffee processed to remove most caffeine, not necessarily all. / latte: Espresso-based drink with milk; proportions depend on the recipe. / espresso: Concentrated coffee brewed by forcing hot water through ground coffee under pressure. / shot: Cafe serving of espresso, with size defined by its recipe. / standard milk: Milk used by default in a particular recipe. / dairy milk: Milk from an animal source rather than a plant-based drink. / plant-based drink: Beverage made from plant ingredients and used as a milk alternative. / syrup: Liquid flavoring or sweetener added to a drink. / modifier: Order detail that changes the standard item. / takeaway: Order intended to be taken away from the premises. / for here: Order intended for consumption on the premises. / read-back: Spoken repetition used to confirm the received order. / clarifying question: Question that resolves a specific uncertainty. / size convention: Local system for naming cup sizes. / order entry: Recording of the selected item and its details. / preparation start: Point at which making the drink begins.
Speaking task: Read Nia's questions and Alex's answers with the cloze words restored. Repeat the final order in this order: size, temperature, coffee choice, milk, syrup, destination. Complete Jo's four-line order and check that decaf did not become a size.
Partner: Nia: Barista / Alex: Customer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Customer Jo calls their usual order regular coffee, then explicitly clarifies that it must be decaf. After separate questions, Jo chooses a large iced latte with oat drink. Use these four confirmed details.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Alex: Thanks. Could you run through the whole order before you send it to the bar?
Nia: Here is the read-back: small hot latte, non-decaf, standard dairy milk, no syrup, takeaway.
Alex: That is exactly right. Just the one drink, please.
Nia: Great. The order entry now matches that. We have not started the drink yet; the bar will use these details.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Lesson 01: Taking a precise drink order

02Comparing drinks without overclaimingLess foam, not more caffeine+

Your goal

Compare drinks on the requested feature while distinguishing texture, espresso serving, and unsupported caffeine claims.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

Less foam, not more caffeine

Customer Morgan is choosing between the small latte and small cappuccino at this fictional cafe. Both recipes use the same espresso serving. The cappuccino has more foam; the latte has more liquid milk. Morgan wants less foam, not an extra espresso serving or a claim about caffeine. Barista Sora must explain the practical difference and recommend the option matching that preference. The recipes describe this cafe only. No laboratory caffeine measurement, universal cup proportion, or health recommendation is supplied.

Morgan: Customer / Sora: Barista

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which feature does Morgan want less of?

Answer and explanation

D. Foam

The brief identifies less foam as the preference to satisfy.

2. What is the same in both recipes?

Answer and explanation

B. The espresso serving

Both small drinks use the same espresso serving in this fictional cafe.

3. Which option matches the stated preference?

Answer and explanation

C. The small latte

The latte has less foam than the cappuccino in the supplied recipes.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

More foam does not mean more espresso. Here, the two small recipes contain the same espresso serving. Match the requested texture without promising an exact caffeine amount or making claims about every cafe.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
cappuccino
Espresso drink with milk and foam, made to a particular cafe recipe.compare the cappuccino
foam
Aerated layer or texture containing bubbles in milk or another liquid.reduce the foam
liquid milk
Milk considered separately from its aerated foam.compare liquid milk
microfoam
Milk foam with very small bubbles and a smooth texture.describe microfoam
texture
How a drink feels in the mouth.compare the texture
mouthfeel
Sensation of a drink in the mouth beyond its flavor.describe the mouthfeel
body
Perceived weight or fullness of a drink.describe the body
flavor intensity
Perceived strength of taste or aroma.compare flavor intensity
caffeine content
Amount of caffeine in a specified quantity or serving.avoid guessing caffeine content
espresso serving
Recipe-defined amount of prepared espresso used in a drink.use the same espresso serving
shot count
Number of recipe-defined espresso servings requested.confirm the shot count
milk proportion
Share of a drink made up by milk.compare the milk proportion
dry dose
Mass of dry ground coffee used for a brew.weigh the dry dose
like-for-like
Comparing items on the same relevant basis.make a like-for-like comparison
beverage yield
Mass of brewed coffee collected, excluding the cup.record beverage yield
aeration
Incorporation of air into a liquid.describe milk aeration
steam wand
Machine part used to introduce steam into milk.identify the steam wand
milk pitcher
Vessel used to hold milk during preparation.identify the milk pitcher
crema
Foam-like surface layer produced on espresso during extraction.distinguish crema from milk foam
extraction
Transfer of soluble coffee compounds into brewing water.discuss extraction
concentration
Amount of a substance relative to the quantity of liquid.distinguish concentration from serving amount
extra shot
Additional espresso serving beyond the recipe.request an extra shot
TDS
Total dissolved solids; a measure of beverage concentration, not a direct caffeine measurement.measure coffee TDS
recipe variation
Difference in formulation between drinks or cafes.allow for recipe variation

Strong can mean intense flavor, concentrated coffee, or high caffeine. Ask which feature matters. A customer asking for less foam is not necessarily asking for a weaker flavor or less caffeine.

C Notice the language

Explain the contrast the customer needs

Specialist vocabulary can help when it answers a specific question. Start with the visible difference, such as more foam or more liquid milk. Add terms like texture only after connecting them to the customer preference. Avoid making taste sound objectively better or worse.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the comparison
Are you comparing our small latte and small cappuccino?
Ask about preference
Is it the foam you would like less of?
State the shared feature
Both use the same espresso serving here.
Explain the cappuccino
Our small cappuccino has more foam.
Explain the latte
Our small latte has more liquid milk.
Recommend on evidence
The latte better matches your preference for less foam.
Limit the claim
I am describing our recipes, not every cafe.
Separate features
Foam and caffeine are different questions.
Clarify strong
By stronger, do you mean flavor or caffeine?
Avoid a false measurement
I do not have a measured caffeine figure for these drinks.
Keep the size consistent
I am comparing the two small sizes.
Confirm no extra shot
You are not asking for an additional espresso serving.
Use accessible language
Texture means how it feels in your mouth.
Separate crema
The espresso surface layer is not the same as milk foam.
Check understanding
Does the less-foamy option sound closer to what you want?
Close the choice
A small latte, based on your preference for less foam.
6 language and precision notes
Comparatives
More foam and less foam require a clear comparison item.
Same versus identical
The same espresso serving does not make every drink feature identical.
Here
This word limits a recipe statement to the actual cafe.
Would suit
A recommendation can match a stated preference without claiming universal superiority.
Strong
Clarify whether the speaker means taste, concentration, or caffeine.
Not necessarily
Use it to reject a false implication without inventing an alternative fact.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which recommendation is best supported?

Answer and explanation

D. Choose the latte if less foam is your priority.

The supplied recipe comparison directly supports the latte for less foam.

2. Which statement preserves scope?

Answer and explanation

C. In our small recipes, the espresso serving is the same.

The phrase limits the comparison to the two actual cafe recipes.

3. What should Sora clarify if asked for stronger?

Answer and explanation

C. Whether Morgan means flavor or caffeine

Strong has several possible meanings that require a specific follow-up question.

4. Which feature is not established?

Answer and explanation

C. Exact caffeine content in milligrams

No measured caffeine quantity is supplied in the brief or recipe comparison.

E Conversations

Less foam, not more caffeine

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Morgan: Customer / Sora: Barista

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • preference
  • milk proportion
  • less foam
  • extra shot
  • same espresso serving
  • like-for-like
  • texture
  • shot count
  • recipe variation
  • stronger
  1. Morgan
    I normally get a cappuccino, but I often leave half the foam. Would a latte suit me better?
  2. Sora
    If you prefer 1, our small latte would be a good choice.
  3. Morgan
    Is that because it has more coffee in it, or is it just the milk?
  4. Sora
    These two small drinks use the 2. The latte has more liquid milk and less foam.
  5. Morgan
    So it feels less airy? I like the milk, just not a thick layer sitting on top.
  6. Sora
    Yes, that is the 3 difference. The cappuccino is the foamier of these two.
  7. Morgan
    People keep telling me cappuccino is stronger. Does that mean another shot?
  8. Sora
    Not in these recipes. By 4, people may mean that the coffee flavor stands out more.
  9. Morgan
    I mean the flavor, really. I do not want you to add extra coffee to make the latte less foamy.
  10. Sora
    I will keep the standard 5. Less foam does not require an extra shot.
  11. Morgan
    Good. Are we comparing the small drinks? The latte I had elsewhere came in a much bigger cup.
  12. Sora
    Yes, this is a 6 comparison: our small latte and our small cappuccino.
  13. Morgan
    That helps. More milk might soften the taste a little, then?
  14. Sora
    The 7 changes the balance. The latte is the milkier option in this comparison.
  15. Morgan
    All right, a small latte sounds closer to what I enjoy. Just the standard version.
  16. Sora
    That fits your 8. No extra coffee and no changes beyond the drink you selected.
  17. Morgan
    At another cafe, should I ask the same question about foam rather than rely on the name?
  18. Sora
    That can help. There is 9 between cafes, especially when you compare different sizes.
  19. Morgan
    Thanks. I will have the small latte here, then. I do not need a caffeine figure.
  20. Sora
    One small latte, with no 10. I will use the standard recipe we have just compared.

Answers and explanations
  1. less foam Turn 2. Less foam is Morgan's stated preference, and the cafe's latte is the closer recipe match.
  2. same espresso serving Turn 4. The supplied small recipes have the same espresso serving; changing foam does not automatically add coffee.
  3. texture Turn 6. Texture describes the feel of the drink, rather than the amount of caffeine it contains.
  4. stronger Turn 8. Stronger can mean perceived flavor intensity or caffeine, so the meaning needs clarification.
  5. shot count Turn 10. Shot count is the espresso-serving quantity, which Morgan does not want increased.
  6. like-for-like Turn 12. Like-for-like compares the specified small drinks at this cafe, not different sizes at different shops.
  7. milk proportion Turn 14. Milk proportion describes the milk share of the drink and helps explain its flavor balance without claiming measured caffeine.
  8. preference Turn 16. Preference is Morgan's choice of a less-foamy drink, not a medical need or a universal quality judgment.
  9. recipe variation Turn 18. Recipe variation means drink names do not guarantee identical proportions at every cafe.
  10. extra shot Turn 20. An extra shot is an additional espresso serving, which has not been requested.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Morgan: I normally get a cappuccino, but I often leave half the foam. Would a latte suit me better?
  2. Sora: If you prefer less foam, our small latte would be a good choice.
  3. Morgan: Is that because it has more coffee in it, or is it just the milk?
  4. Sora: These two small drinks use the same espresso serving. The latte has more liquid milk and less foam.
  5. Morgan: So it feels less airy? I like the milk, just not a thick layer sitting on top.
  6. Sora: Yes, that is the texture difference. The cappuccino is the foamier of these two.
  7. Morgan: People keep telling me cappuccino is stronger. Does that mean another shot?
  8. Sora: Not in these recipes. By stronger, people may mean that the coffee flavor stands out more.
  9. Morgan: I mean the flavor, really. I do not want you to add extra coffee to make the latte less foamy.
  10. Sora: I will keep the standard shot count. Less foam does not require an extra shot.
  11. Morgan: Good. Are we comparing the small drinks? The latte I had elsewhere came in a much bigger cup.
  12. Sora: Yes, this is a like-for-like comparison: our small latte and our small cappuccino.
  13. Morgan: That helps. More milk might soften the taste a little, then?
  14. Sora: The milk proportion changes the balance. The latte is the milkier option in this comparison.
  15. Morgan: All right, a small latte sounds closer to what I enjoy. Just the standard version.
  16. Sora: That fits your preference. No extra coffee and no changes beyond the drink you selected.
  17. Morgan: At another cafe, should I ask the same question about foam rather than rely on the name?
  18. Sora: That can help. There is recipe variation between cafes, especially when you compare different sizes.
  19. Morgan: Thanks. I will have the small latte here, then. I do not need a caffeine figure.
  20. Sora: One small latte, with no extra shot. I will use the standard recipe we have just compared.
Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Less foam, not more coffee

The small-drink comparison in the case.

  1. Customer: Which has more coffee, your small latte or small cappuccino? I dislike too much foam.
  2. Barista: Both use the same espresso serving here, but their milk and foam balance differs.
  3. Customer: Then choosing the latte wouldn't automatically mean ordering a different espresso amount at this cafe?
  4. Barista: Correct. Our small latte has more liquid milk, while the cappuccino has more foam.
  5. Customer: The latte sounds closer to what I mean. I want something milky, not especially foamy.
  6. Barista: Would you like the small latte, then, with its usual milk balance and no additions?
  7. Customer: Yes, please. Does a drink tasting stronger always mean it contains more caffeine?
  8. Barista: Not necessarily. Taste alone doesn't establish caffeine content, and I don't have exact figures.
  9. Customer: That's enough information. Please keep the standard coffee recipe rather than adding an extra shot.
  10. Barista: Certainly. One small latte with the standard recipe; no extra shot requested.
An Americano is not filter coffee

A customer wants the brewed-coffee option on the menu.

  1. Customer: I'd like brewed filter coffee. Is that the same menu item as an Americano?
  2. Barista: They're different here. The Americano starts with espresso and water; filter coffee uses our filter brew.
  3. Customer: I see. Which one comes from the batch you have on the counter now?
  4. Barista: The house filter comes from that batch. The Americano is a separate drink order.
  5. Customer: Then I'll choose the house filter. Is milk automatically included in the cup?
  6. Barista: No, we can offer it separately if you'd like. Would you prefer coffee alone?
  7. Customer: Coffee alone, please. I mainly wanted to avoid ordering an espresso-based drink by mistake.
  8. Barista: Understood. A small house filter, black, with no added milk or syrup on this order.
  9. Customer: That works. Could you tell me which bean is being used for today's filter?
  10. Barista: The board names today's house blend. I'll show you its current description beside the price.
Strong can mean flavor

A barista clarifies a taste preference without caffeine claims.

  1. Barista: When you ask for stronger coffee, do you mean a more pronounced flavor or more caffeine?
  2. Customer: A more pronounced flavor. I usually find my drink tastes mostly of milk.
  3. Barista: Thanks. We can compare our smaller milk drink with your usual large latte.
  4. Customer: Would that be a comparison of the milk balance rather than a guaranteed caffeine increase?
  5. Barista: Exactly. The menu descriptions can help with flavor balance, but I can't promise your perception.
  6. Customer: I'd like to hear the smaller option's description and price before changing my order.
  7. Barista: This one has less milk in our recipe and costs four dollars today.
  8. Customer: I'll try that. Please keep the standard recipe rather than invent a special strength setting.
  9. Barista: Certainly. I'll enter the named menu drink so the bar follows the correct recipe.
  10. Customer: Thank you. I meant stronger in taste, and that distinction helped me choose.

Less foam, not more caffeine

  2. Swap roles and say Morgan's stronger question followed by Sora's clarification.

Answer and explanation

Vocabulary: cappuccino: Espresso drink with milk and foam, made to a particular cafe recipe. / foam: Aerated layer or texture containing bubbles in milk or another liquid. / liquid milk: Milk considered separately from its aerated foam. / microfoam: Milk foam with very small bubbles and a smooth texture. / texture: How a drink feels in the mouth. / mouthfeel: Sensation of a drink in the mouth beyond its flavor. / body: Perceived weight or fullness of a drink. / flavor intensity: Perceived strength of taste or aroma. / caffeine content: Amount of caffeine in a specified quantity or serving. / espresso serving: Recipe-defined amount of prepared espresso used in a drink. / shot count: Number of recipe-defined espresso servings requested. / milk proportion: Share of a drink made up by milk. / dry dose: Mass of dry ground coffee used for a brew. / like-for-like: Comparing items on the same relevant basis. / beverage yield: Mass of brewed coffee collected, excluding the cup. / aeration: Incorporation of air into a liquid. / steam wand: Machine part used to introduce steam into milk. / milk pitcher: Vessel used to hold milk during preparation. / crema: Foam-like surface layer produced on espresso during extraction. / extraction: Transfer of soluble coffee compounds into brewing water. / concentration: Amount of a substance relative to the quantity of liquid. / extra shot: Additional espresso serving beyond the recipe. / TDS: Total dissolved solids; a measure of beverage concentration, not a direct caffeine measurement. / recipe variation: Difference in formulation between drinks or cafes.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

03Clarifying milk, syrup, and allergy questionsOat does not settle the question+

Your goal

Acknowledge an allergy, preserve the separate modification, and route the actual product and preparation questions for checking.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

Oat does not settle the question

Customer Priya reports a milk allergy and asks for an oat latte without vanilla syrup. Barista Leo knows oat drink is available but has not checked the current carton ingredients or the shared-equipment arrangements. Shift lead Amina is available to review the request through the cafe allergy procedure. No drink has been prepared or consumed, and no reaction is reported. Leo must communicate what needs checking without claiming that oat, plant-based, or no vanilla automatically establishes suitability.

Priya: Customer / Leo: Barista

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which allergy is reported?

Answer and explanation

B. Milk

Priya explicitly reports a milk allergy, separate from the syrup omission.

2. What has Leo verified so far?

Answer and explanation

A. Oat drink is available

Availability is known, while ingredient and preparation checks remain incomplete.

3. Who can review the request?

Answer and explanation

C. Amina, the shift lead

Amina is the named available lead for the cafe allergy procedure.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

No vanilla is an omission instruction. Milk allergy is a safety-related disclosure. Keep both in the message; neither replaces the need to check the actual oat product and preparation arrangements.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
food allergy
Immune-system reaction to a food that can cause serious harm.report a food allergy
milk allergy
Allergy to milk proteins, not simply a preference against milk.communicate a milk allergy
allergen
Substance capable of triggering an allergic reaction.identify the reported allergen
cross-contact
Unintended introduction of an allergen into food or drink.assess cross-contact
ingredient list
Product information identifying its ingredients.check the ingredient list
current carton
Actual carton being used now, not an earlier product version.check the current carton
product formulation
Ingredients and composition of a particular product.verify the product formulation
supplier change
Change in the source or supplied product.check after a supplier change
shared equipment
Equipment used for more than one product or ingredient type.check shared equipment
preparation arrangement
Actual method and controls used to prepare an order.verify preparation arrangements
allergy procedure
Workplace process for receiving and handling allergy requests.follow the allergy procedure
shift lead
Person leading the team during the current shift.consult the shift lead
suitability
Whether a product and its preparation meet the stated need.avoid assuming suitability
availability
Whether an item can currently be supplied.confirm availability
oat drink
Beverage made using oats, with other ingredients varying by product.check the oat drink
vanilla syrup
Vanilla-flavored liquid ingredient whose formulation must be checked.omit vanilla syrup
omission
Instruction to leave out a specified component.record the omission
contains statement
Label statement identifying specified allergens in a product.review the contains statement
advisory statement
Precautionary label information about possible allergen presence.read the advisory statement
milk protein
Protein from milk that may be relevant to milk allergy.identify milk proteins
ingredient verification
Checking the relevant information for the actual product.complete ingredient verification
allergy flag
Record or signal identifying an allergy-related order.preserve the allergy flag
unverified
Not yet established by the required check.mark suitability unverified
customer update
Message explaining the current status and next step.give a customer update

Plant-based and oat name a product category, not a completed assessment of the drink. A familiar brand, an old carton, or the absence of a remembered warning cannot substitute for current relevant information and actual controls.

C Notice the language

A clear limitation is respectful

A customer may hear yes, we have oat as yes, this is suitable for you. State availability and suitability separately. Acknowledge the allergy directly and explain who will check the actual ingredients and preparation arrangements, without asking the customer to approve an unknown risk.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the allergy
Thank you for telling me about your milk allergy.
Keep the modification
You also want no vanilla syrup.
State availability
We have oat drink available.
Separate suitability
Availability does not confirm suitability for your allergy.
Name the first check
I need to check the current product information.
Name the second check
We also need to check our preparation arrangements.
Identify shared equipment
I have not yet verified the shared-equipment arrangements.
Involve the lead
I will ask Amina to review this through our allergy procedure.
Avoid an assurance
I cannot confirm this drink is suitable yet.
Keep the request together
Oat latte, no vanilla, reported milk allergy.
Explain the pause
We need the relevant checks before making a suitability statement.
Avoid an old label
We need information for the product in use now.
Reject a shortcut
Leaving out syrup does not settle the milk-allergy question.
Give a status update
The request is being checked; there is no confirmed outcome yet.
Promise communication
I will return with the result of the check.
Preserve the boundary
I will not describe an unchecked drink as allergy-safe.
6 language and precision notes
Available versus suitable
Availability concerns supply; suitability needs the relevant assessment.
Also
Use also to preserve the syrup instruction without replacing the allergy disclosure.
Yet
Not confirmed yet states an unresolved status rather than a final refusal or approval.
Current
This word identifies the actual product information needed now.
Reported allergy
Report the customer's words accurately without diagnosing or minimizing them.
Check with
Name the person and question, rather than offering an unexplained delay.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response is best?

Answer and explanation

C. We have oat drink, but I need to check the product and preparation for your milk allergy.

The answer separates stock availability from ingredient and preparation suitability.

2. What does no vanilla establish?

Answer and explanation

D. A request to omit that syrup

The omission concerns one ingredient and does not settle the full allergy question.

3. Which summary should reach Amina?

Answer and explanation

C. Oat latte, no vanilla, reported milk allergy; relevant checks pending

The summary preserves all known details and the unresolved checking status.

4. Which claim is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

B. The complete drink is suitable for Priya's allergy.

Neither current product information nor preparation arrangements have yet been verified.

Answers and explanations
  1. milk allergy Turn 2. Milk allergy is the reported safety-related information, separate from the customer's syrup preference.
  2. availability Turn 4. Availability means the product is present; it does not establish suitability for the reported allergy.
  3. ingredient list Turn 6. The ingredient list supplies product composition information for the actual carton rather than assumptions from its name.
  4. shared equipment Turn 8. Shared equipment is relevant because preparation can introduce allergens even when a product name sounds suitable.
  5. omission Turn 10. Omission refers to leaving out vanilla syrup, a separate instruction that does not resolve the milk-allergy question.
  6. shift lead Turn 12. The shift lead is the named available person who can review the request through the cafe process.
  7. allergy flag Turn 14. The allergy flag preserves the disclosure as allergy-related information rather than reducing it to a preference.
  8. suitability Turn 16. Suitability is the unresolved question about the complete product and preparation, not merely whether oat drink is stocked.
  9. customer update Turn 18. A customer update communicates the review result or continuing uncertainty without implying that a pending check is complete.
  10. allergy procedure Turn 20. The allergy procedure is the actual workplace route for the request; this conversation does not substitute for its required checks.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Oat is a choice, not a guarantee

The oat-latte question from the case.

  1. Customer: I have a milk allergy. Could I have an oat latte without vanilla syrup?
  2. Barista: I can note those choices, but I need to check the ingredients and equipment arrangements.
  3. Customer: Isn't using oat drink enough to answer that? I thought it replaced all dairy milk.
  4. Barista: It replaces the milk choice, but doesn't settle every ingredient or cross-contact question.
  5. Customer: Then please ask whoever can explain those details before you start making the drink.
  6. Barista: I'll ask our lead and keep the drink unmade while they check with you.
  7. Customer: Please also make sure the no-vanilla request stays attached to my order afterward.
  8. Barista: Certainly. That's separate from the allergy question; I won't lose that preference.
  9. Customer: Could I see the current oat carton information while we're waiting for the lead?
  10. Barista: I'll ask the lead to show you the product information and explain the remaining questions.
No syrup doesn't describe the carton

A customer clarifies what they mean by less sweet.

  1. Barista: You asked for unsweetened. Do you mean no syrup, or a particular milk product too?
  2. Customer: I mainly mean no syrup. I don't know whether the oat drink itself is sweetened.
  3. Barista: I'll show you the oat drink's ingredients. A milk alternative can contain sweeteners of its own.
  4. Customer: Please do. I'm choosing by taste, not asking you to make a medical recommendation.
  5. Barista: Understood. We can compare the listed options without promising how sweet each will taste to you.
  6. Customer: Then leave out the vanilla syrup and keep the oat product shown on this carton.
  7. Barista: I'll enter no vanilla syrup, not describe the complete drink as having no sweeteners.
  8. Customer: That's the distinction I needed. Please keep the drink size and coffee choice unchanged.
  9. Barista: Medium hot latte with oat drink, no vanilla syrup; the other choices stay as ordered.
  10. Customer: Yes. That gives me the change I asked for without guessing about the whole recipe.
Checking the actual product

A barista asks the lead about a customer's ingredient question.

  1. Barista: The customer asks whether our chocolate sauce contains milk. I haven't answered from memory.
  2. Lead: Which product are we using today? A similar bottle was delivered with a different label.
  3. Barista: This is the bottle at the bar, but I haven't compared its information with the delivery.
  4. Lead: Let's check this exact product. A previous brand's ingredient list won't settle today's question.
  5. Barista: The customer hasn't said whether it's an allergy or a preference. Shall I ask?
  6. Lead: Yes, ask directly and relay their words. Keep the ingredient check underway as well.
  7. Barista: I'll say we're checking today's sauce, not that removing whipped cream answers the question.
  8. Lead: Good. The topping and sauce are separate components, so both need their own accurate description.
  9. Barista: I'll leave the drink pending and bring back the customer's stated concern to you.
  10. Lead: Then I can discuss the current information with them before they decide what to order.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Oat does not settle the question
  1. Read the allergy exchange, keeping milk allergy distinct from no vanilla.
  2. Repeat Leo's handoff to Amina using all three request details.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Keep a new allergy request intact

Customer Noor requests a soy latte without caramel and reports a sesame allergy. Lead Rosa is available. Product and preparation checks are incomplete.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Barista: "The reported allergy is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. sesame

Sesame is explicitly reported and must not be replaced by soy.

2. Customer: "Please leave out ___."

Answer and explanation

A. caramel

Caramel is the separate omission requested for this drink.

3. Barista: "The lead available to review this is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. Rosa

Rosa is the named available reviewer in the new scenario.

4. Barista: "Suitability remains ___."

Answer and explanation

C. unconfirmed

Both relevant checks are incomplete, so suitability cannot be asserted.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 03: Clarifying milk, syrup, and allergy questions
Original fictional case: Customer Priya reports a milk allergy and asks for an oat latte without vanilla syrup. Barista Leo knows oat drink is available but has not checked the current carton ingredients or the shared-equipment arrangements. Shift lead Amina is available to review the request through the cafe allergy procedure. No drink has been prepared or consumed, and no reaction is reported. Leo must communicate what needs checking without claiming that oat, plant-based, or no vanilla automatically establishes suitability.
Language workshop: Oat does not settle the question
Communication goal: Acknowledge an allergy, preserve the separate modification, and route the actual product and preparation questions for checking.
Language guidance: No vanilla is an omission instruction. Milk allergy is a safety-related disclosure. Keep both in the message; neither replaces the need to check the actual oat product and preparation arrangements.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Thank you for telling me about your milk allergy. / You also want no vanilla syrup. / We have oat drink available. / Availability does not confirm suitability for your allergy. / I need to check the current product information. / We also need to check our preparation arrangements. / I have not yet verified the shared-equipment arrangements. / I will ask Amina to review this through our allergy procedure. / I cannot confirm this drink is suitable yet. / Oat latte, no vanilla, reported milk allergy. / We need the relevant checks before making a suitability statement. / We need information for the product in use now. / Leaving out syrup does not settle the milk-allergy question. / The request is being checked; there is no confirmed outcome yet. / I will return with the result of the check. / I will not describe an unchecked drink as allergy-safe.
Vocabulary: food allergy: Immune-system reaction to a food that can cause serious harm. / milk allergy: Allergy to milk proteins, not simply a preference against milk. / allergen: Substance capable of triggering an allergic reaction. / cross-contact: Unintended introduction of an allergen into food or drink. / ingredient list: Product information identifying its ingredients. / current carton: Actual carton being used now, not an earlier product version. / product formulation: Ingredients and composition of a particular product. / supplier change: Change in the source or supplied product. / shared equipment: Equipment used for more than one product or ingredient type. / preparation arrangement: Actual method and controls used to prepare an order. / allergy procedure: Workplace process for receiving and handling allergy requests. / shift lead: Person leading the team during the current shift. / suitability: Whether a product and its preparation meet the stated need. / availability: Whether an item can currently be supplied. / oat drink: Beverage made using oats, with other ingredients varying by product. / vanilla syrup: Vanilla-flavored liquid ingredient whose formulation must be checked. / omission: Instruction to leave out a specified component. / contains statement: Label statement identifying specified allergens in a product. / advisory statement: Precautionary label information about possible allergen presence. / milk protein: Protein from milk that may be relevant to milk allergy. / ingredient verification: Checking the relevant information for the actual product. / allergy flag: Record or signal identifying an allergy-related order. / unverified: Not yet established by the required check. / customer update: Message explaining the current status and next step.
Speaking task: Read the allergy exchange, keeping milk allergy distinct from no vanilla. Repeat Leo's handoff to Amina using all three request details. Complete Noor's transfer and check the allergen, omission, lead, and unresolved status.
Partner: Priya: Customer / Leo: Barista
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.

Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Priya: Understood. Nothing has been made for me yet, so please come back with the result before we go any further.
Leo: I will give you a customer update after the review. If something remains unknown, I will say that clearly instead of filling in the gap.
Priya: Thank you. I will wait here while you ask her.
Leo: You are welcome. I will now take the full request to Amina under our allergy procedure, keeping the allergy and the separate no-vanilla instruction together.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
