04Managing the queue and pickupTwo customers named Sam+

Your goal

Interrupt a mistaken pickup politely, match the ticket and drink, and explain readiness without inventing a wait.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

Two customers named Sam

Two customers at the cafe are named Sam. Ticket 64 is a small hot latte and is complete. Ticket 67 is a large iced decaf latte and is still in progress. The Sam holding ticket 67 hears the name called and reaches toward the completed cup. Barista Hana notices before the cup is collected. No one has consumed the drink. Hana needs to clarify the ticket number and drink details, preserve the completed drink for the correct customer, and explain that ticket 67 is not ready. No reliable wait estimate is supplied.

Sam: Customer holding ticket 67 / Hana: Pickup barista

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which ticket belongs to this Sam?

Answer and explanation

C. 67

The brief explicitly identifies this customer as holding ticket 67.

2. Which drink is complete?

Answer and explanation

C. Ticket 64, small hot latte

Ticket 64 is complete while the other drink remains in progress.

3. What wait can Hana promise?

Answer and explanation

B. No precise wait is established

The brief provides no reliable estimate for completion of ticket 67.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A ready call applies to the identified drink, not everyone with the same name. Add the ticket number and key drink details. Keep ready for ticket 64 separate from in progress for ticket 67.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
pickup counter
Area where prepared orders are collected.wait at the pickup counter
ticket number
Number used to identify an order.confirm the ticket number
order name
Customer name attached to an order.call the order name
duplicate name
Name shared by more than one current customer.resolve a duplicate name
completed drink
Drink whose preparation is finished.identify the completed drink
in progress
Started or underway but not yet finished.mark the order in progress
ready call
Announcement that a specified item can be collected.make a clear ready call
drink description
Identifying details such as size, temperature, and coffee choice.repeat the drink description
cup label
Information attached to a cup to identify its order.read the cup label
collection
Taking possession of the prepared order.confirm before collection
order match
Agreement between the customer order and prepared item.verify the order match
queue
Line or sequence of customers or pending orders.manage the queue
queue position
Place in a waiting or production sequence.explain queue position
preparation status
Current stage of making an item.check preparation status
partial order
Order with some but not all items complete.identify a partial order
estimated wait
Approximate expected remaining delay.check the estimated wait
time promise
Commitment about when an item will be ready.avoid an unsupported time promise
pickup mix-up
Confusion about which customer should collect an item.prevent a pickup mix-up
identifier
Detail used to distinguish one order from another.use a second identifier
readiness
State of being ready for the relevant next step.confirm readiness
handover point
Place or stage where an item passes to the customer.check at the handover point
collection check
Verification that the correct order is being collected.complete the collection check
pending item
Item that has not yet reached the required finished state.identify the pending item
polite interruption
Brief respectful intervention to prevent a misunderstanding.use a polite interruption

Order sequence does not always give a reliable completion time. Different drinks can take different paths through the bar. Explain the verified status and check an estimate instead of converting a queue position into a promise.

C Notice the language

Correct the match without blaming the person

A shared name makes a mix-up understandable. Use a brief interruption, then give the identifying detail that resolves it. Do not call the customer careless. A number plus drink description is clearer than repeating the same name more loudly.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Interrupt briefly
One moment, please; let me check the ticket.
Ask for the identifier
What number is on your receipt?
Identify the completed cup
This is ticket 64, the small hot latte.
Name the shared-name issue
We have two orders under Sam.
Confirm the other order
Yours is ticket 67, the large iced decaf latte.
State the status
That drink is still in progress.
Avoid a false wait
I do not have a checked wait estimate yet.
Use both identifiers
I will call the number and drink as well as the name.
Prevent collection
Please wait while I match this cup to the order.
Acknowledge the confusion
I understand why you thought that call was yours.
Keep the correct cup
This completed drink belongs to the other ticket.
Check before announcing
I will confirm readiness before calling it.
Explain the next update
I can check with the bar for an estimate.
Separate the orders
One is complete; the other is still being made.
Repeat key modifiers
Large, iced, and decaf are all on ticket 67.
Close the correction
Thank you; we have matched the right order now.
6 language and precision notes
One moment
A concise interruption can prevent a wrong handover without sounding accusatory.
This versus yours
Name the ticket after each pronoun when two orders are close together.
Still
Still in progress preserves the current unfinished state.
Ready for whom
A readiness statement needs the correct order identifier.
Yet
No estimate yet does not mean immediate service or an unlimited delay.
Number clarity
Repeat sixty-seven distinctly from sixty-four rather than saying the last digit alone.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which call is least ambiguous?

Answer and explanation

D. Sam, ticket 64, small hot latte

The number and description distinguish two orders with the same customer name.

2. Which status report is correct?

Answer and explanation

A. Ticket 64 is complete; ticket 67 is in progress.

The report preserves the separately established preparation states.

3. Which response invents a fact?

Answer and explanation

D. Yours will definitely be ready in thirty seconds.

No reliable completion estimate is supplied, so thirty seconds is unsupported.

4. Why check before collection?

Answer and explanation

C. The name alone does not distinguish the orders.

Both customers share a name, requiring additional order details to match correctly.

Check explanations in the book, page 91 ↗

E Conversations

Two customers named Sam

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Sam: Customer holding ticket 67 / Hana: Pickup barista

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • handover point
  • drink description
  • ready call
  • pickup mix-up
  • in progress
  • estimated wait
  • cup label
  • completed drink
  • sixty-four
  • ticket number
  1. Sam
    I heard Sam called. Is this my latte? I have been waiting near the counter, and I thought that must be mine.
  2. Hana
    One moment, please. Could I check your 1 before you take it? We have two customers named Sam waiting for different drinks.
  3. Sam
    Of course. My receipt says sixty-seven. I ordered the large iced decaf latte, so perhaps this is the other person's cup.
  4. Hana
    Thank you. This completed cup is 2, a small hot latte. Yours is sixty-seven, so I want to keep the two orders separate.
  5. Sam
    Oh, I see. Mine should be larger and iced. I just heard my name and came forward.
  6. Hana
    That is understandable. Your 3 is large iced decaf latte. Those details do not match the small hot drink that was called.
  7. Sam
    Is mine finished somewhere else on the counter, then? I do not want to stand in the wrong place or miss another call.
  8. Hana
    Your drink is still 4. I have checked the current status, and it has not reached the pickup stage yet.
  9. Sam
    Do you have a rough wait for mine? I need to know whether I have time to stay.
  10. Hana
    I do not have an 5 to give you yet. I can check with the bar, but I do not want to invent a time.
  11. Sam
    That is fair. When you call it, could you say the number too? Otherwise we might both come forward again.
  12. Hana
    Certainly. I will include the number and drink in the 6, not just Sam. That will make the collection clearer for both of you.
  13. Sam
    I have not taken this one. I will leave it for the other Sam.
  14. Hana
    Thank you. The 7 belongs to that ticket. I will keep it matched to its order rather than treating the shared name as enough.
  15. Sam
    And mine is definitely still marked decaf? That is one of the details I particularly wanted to make sure had not been lost.
  16. Hana
    Yes. The 8 for your order retains large, iced, and decaf. We are clarifying collection, not changing those choices.
  17. Sam
    Thanks. I will listen for sixty-seven. Please include decaf when you call it.
  18. Hana
    Exactly. At the 9, we can match the number and drink before collection. That avoids relying on a name that two people share.
  19. Sam
    Thank you for catching it. I would have been surprised to walk away with a small hot drink after ordering something quite different.
  20. Hana
    You are welcome. We have resolved the 10, and yours remains in progress. I will make the next call clear once that drink is actually ready.

Answers and explanations
  1. ticket number Turn 2. The ticket number distinguishes the two customers sharing the name Sam before the drink is collected.
  2. sixty-four Turn 4. Sixty-four identifies the finished small hot latte, not Sam's large iced decaf order.
  3. drink description Turn 6. The drink description adds size, temperature, and coffee choice to the ticket identifier, making the match clearer.
  4. in progress Turn 8. In progress is the verified unfinished preparation state of ticket 67; it is not a ready-for-collection announcement.
  5. estimated wait Turn 10. An estimated wait requires a current check; Hana has no reliable time to give at this point.
  6. ready call Turn 12. The ready call will identify the specific completed order, reducing the ambiguity caused by a shared name.
  7. completed drink Turn 14. The completed drink is ticket 64's small hot latte; it remains separate from the pending ticket 67.
  8. cup label Turn 16. The cup label carries the order details; the check confirms the requested decaf modifier remains attached to ticket 67.
  9. handover point Turn 18. The handover point is where the final match matters before the prepared drink passes to the customer.
  10. pickup mix-up Turn 20. The pickup mix-up concerns identity at collection; resolving it does not mean the pending drink has become ready.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Sam: I heard Sam called. Is this my latte? I have been waiting near the counter, and I thought that must be mine.
  2. Hana: One moment, please. Could I check your ticket number before you take it? We have two customers named Sam waiting for different drinks.
  3. Sam: Of course. My receipt says sixty-seven. I ordered the large iced decaf latte, so perhaps this is the other person's cup.
  4. Hana: Thank you. This completed cup is sixty-four, a small hot latte. Yours is sixty-seven, so I want to keep the two orders separate.
  5. Sam: Oh, I see. Mine should be larger and iced. I just heard my name and came forward.
  6. Hana: That is understandable. Your drink description is large iced decaf latte. Those details do not match the small hot drink that was called.
  7. Sam: Is mine finished somewhere else on the counter, then? I do not want to stand in the wrong place or miss another call.
  8. Hana: Your drink is still in progress. I have checked the current status, and it has not reached the pickup stage yet.
  9. Sam: Do you have a rough wait for mine? I need to know whether I have time to stay.
  10. Hana: I do not have an estimated wait to give you yet. I can check with the bar, but I do not want to invent a time.
  11. Sam: That is fair. When you call it, could you say the number too? Otherwise we might both come forward again.
  12. Hana: Certainly. I will include the number and drink in the ready call, not just Sam. That will make the collection clearer for both of you.
  13. Sam: I have not taken this one. I will leave it for the other Sam.
  14. Hana: Thank you. The completed drink belongs to that ticket. I will keep it matched to its order rather than treating the shared name as enough.
  15. Sam: And mine is definitely still marked decaf? That is one of the details I particularly wanted to make sure had not been lost.
  16. Hana: Yes. The cup label for your order retains large, iced, and decaf. We are clarifying collection, not changing those choices.
  17. Sam: Thanks. I will listen for sixty-seven. Please include decaf when you call it.
  18. Hana: Exactly. At the handover point, we can match the number and drink before collection. That avoids relying on a name that two people share.
  19. Sam: Thank you for catching it. I would have been surprised to walk away with a small hot drink after ordering something quite different.
  20. Hana: You are welcome. We have resolved the pickup mix-up, and yours remains in progress. I will make the next call clear once that drink is actually ready.
Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Two Sams at the counter

The duplicated pickup name in the case.

  1. Customer: You called Sam. Is this my large iced decaf latte on ticket sixty-seven?
  2. Barista: This is a small hot latte on sixty-four, for another customer named Sam.
  3. Customer: Thanks for checking. I heard my name and didn't look closely at the drink.
  4. Barista: Your iced decaf is still in progress. I'll call Sam and sixty-seven together.
  5. Customer: Has my mobile order reached the bar, or should I show you the confirmation again?
  6. Barista: It's on the order queue as sixty-seven. You don't need to submit it again.
  7. Customer: All right. Can I wait over here without blocking the drinks being collected?
  8. Barista: Yes, that space is clear. I'll call sixty-seven and describe the drink as well.
  9. Customer: Please do. Two identical names made me think the first call was mine.
  10. Barista: Here's sixty-seven now: large iced decaf latte. Please compare that with your ticket before collection.
Received doesn't mean ready

A customer interprets a mobile confirmation.

  1. Customer: My phone says order received, but I can't see the drinks on the shelf.
  2. Barista: Received means the order reached us. It doesn't yet mean both drinks are ready.
  3. Customer: I thought the confirmation was a collection message. Can you check its current status?
  4. Barista: Certainly. What's the order number shown on that confirmation, rather than your payment details?
  5. Customer: It's eighty-five, under Morgan. There should be two drinks and one plain pastry.
  6. Barista: I see it. One drink is complete, while the second drink and pastry are pending.
  7. Customer: Would you like me to take the finished drink now and return for the rest?
  8. Barista: We can keep the order together if you prefer; tell me which arrangement works for you.
  9. Customer: Together, please. I'd rather collect once than risk forgetting one of the items.
  10. Barista: I'll keep the completed drink linked to eighty-five and call when the full order is ready.
A courier has a different number

A counter worker resolves a pickup-identifier mismatch.

  1. Courier: I'm collecting order thirty-two, but the bag here shows the customer's name without that number.
  2. Counter worker: Let's match the order before handing it over. What identifier does your pickup screen show?
  3. Courier: It shows cafe order one-thirty-two. I shortened that when I first spoke to you.
  4. Counter worker: Thanks. We also have a separate thirty-two, so the full number makes a difference.
  5. Courier: The one-thirty-two order should have three drinks. I don't need the other customer's details.
  6. Counter worker: Correct. This grouped order has three drinks and the matching full reference on its ticket.
  7. Courier: Can you confirm all three are part of this pickup, rather than separate counter orders?
  8. Counter worker: Yes, the ticket lists three, and all three are linked to one-thirty-two here.
  9. Courier: That matches my screen. I'll use the full number when I confirm collection.
  10. Counter worker: Thank you. I'll record this order as collected, leaving thirty-two's status unchanged.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Two customers named Sam
  1. Read the pickup exchange, clearly separating sixty-four from sixty-seven.
  2. Repeat the two ticket descriptions with their different readiness states.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Separate two orders under Lee

Ticket 81 is a completed small tea. Ticket 84 is a large iced coffee still in progress. Both orders use Lee. The customer in front of you holds ticket 84.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Barista: "Your ticket number is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. 84

The customer in this exchange holds ticket 84, not 81.

2. Customer: "I ordered the large iced ___."

Answer and explanation

C. coffee

Coffee is the drink attached to ticket 84 in the brief.

3. Barista: "The completed small tea belongs to ticket ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 81

Ticket 81 identifies the completed tea, a separate order.

4. Barista: "Your order remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. in progress

Ticket 84 has not reached completion in the supplied facts.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 04: Managing the queue and pickup
Original fictional case: Two customers at the cafe are named Sam. Ticket 64 is a small hot latte and is complete. Ticket 67 is a large iced decaf latte and is still in progress. The Sam holding ticket 67 hears the name called and reaches toward the completed cup. Barista Hana notices before the cup is collected. No one has consumed the drink. Hana needs to clarify the ticket number and drink details, preserve the completed drink for the correct customer, and explain that ticket 67 is not ready. No reliable wait estimate is supplied.
Language workshop: Two customers named Sam
Communication goal: Interrupt a mistaken pickup politely, match the ticket and drink, and explain readiness without inventing a wait.
Language guidance: A ready call applies to the identified drink, not everyone with the same name. Add the ticket number and key drink details. Keep ready for ticket 64 separate from in progress for ticket 67.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): One moment, please; let me check the ticket. / What number is on your receipt? / This is ticket 64, the small hot latte. / We have two orders under Sam. / Yours is ticket 67, the large iced decaf latte. / That drink is still in progress. / I do not have a checked wait estimate yet. / I will call the number and drink as well as the name. / Please wait while I match this cup to the order. / I understand why you thought that call was yours. / This completed drink belongs to the other ticket. / I will confirm readiness before calling it. / I can check with the bar for an estimate. / One is complete; the other is still being made. / Large, iced, and decaf are all on ticket 67. / Thank you; we have matched the right order now.
Vocabulary: pickup counter: Area where prepared orders are collected. / ticket number: Number used to identify an order. / order name: Customer name attached to an order. / duplicate name: Name shared by more than one current customer. / completed drink: Drink whose preparation is finished. / in progress: Started or underway but not yet finished. / ready call: Announcement that a specified item can be collected. / drink description: Identifying details such as size, temperature, and coffee choice. / cup label: Information attached to a cup to identify its order. / collection: Taking possession of the prepared order. / order match: Agreement between the customer order and prepared item. / queue: Line or sequence of customers or pending orders. / queue position: Place in a waiting or production sequence. / preparation status: Current stage of making an item. / partial order: Order with some but not all items complete. / estimated wait: Approximate expected remaining delay. / time promise: Commitment about when an item will be ready. / pickup mix-up: Confusion about which customer should collect an item. / identifier: Detail used to distinguish one order from another. / readiness: State of being ready for the relevant next step. / handover point: Place or stage where an item passes to the customer. / collection check: Verification that the correct order is being collected. / pending item: Item that has not yet reached the required finished state. / polite interruption: Brief respectful intervention to prevent a misunderstanding.
Speaking task: Read the pickup exchange, clearly separating sixty-four from sixty-seven. Repeat the two ticket descriptions with their different readiness states. Complete the new pickup check and retain every identifier in the supplied case.
Partner: Sam: Customer holding ticket 67 / Hana: Pickup barista
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Ticket 81 is a completed small tea. Ticket 84 is a large iced coffee still in progress. Both orders use Lee. The customer in front of you holds ticket 84.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Sam: Thanks. I will listen for sixty-seven. Please include decaf when you call it.
Hana: Exactly. At the handover point, we can match the number and drink before collection. That avoids relying on a name that two people share.
Sam: Thank you for catching it. I would have been surprised to walk away with a small hot drink after ordering something quite different.
Hana: You are welcome. We have resolved the pickup mix-up, and yours remains in progress. I will make the next call clear once that drink is actually ready.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

05Handling remakes and mistaken labelsThe label is not the ticket+

Your goal

Identify an order-label mismatch, request a checked remake, and keep timing and payment decisions separate.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

The label is not the ticket

Ticket 22 requests a small iced latte with no syrup. Its cup label says hot vanilla latte. The customer has not collected the drink. Counter worker Ben catches the mismatch and asks barista Imani to arrange a replacement matching the ticket. A remake is available, but the current wait has not been checked. No refund, complimentary extra, or queue priority has been authorized. The conversation concerns correcting and verifying the order, not guessing how the mismatch happened or merely relabeling an unchecked drink.

Ben: Counter worker / Imani: Barista

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does ticket 22 request?

Answer and explanation

D. Small iced latte with no syrup

The ticket gives the intended size, temperature, and syrup omission.

2. What is wrong with the cup label?

Answer and explanation

C. It says hot vanilla latte

Hot and vanilla conflict with iced and no syrup on the ticket.

3. Which outcome is not established?

Answer and explanation

C. A checked remake wait

A remake is available, but nobody has checked its current wait.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A label can be wrong even when the intended order is clear. Compare it with the source ticket. Changing the label alone does not verify what is in the cup or complete the requested remake.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
remake
New preparation intended to replace an incorrect or unsatisfactory item.request a remake
replacement
Item supplied instead of another item.arrange a replacement
mismatch
Difference between records or items that should agree.identify a mismatch
source ticket
Original order record used to verify the intended drink.check the source ticket
incorrect label
Cup information that does not match the intended order.correct an incorrect label
order specification
Complete stated details of the requested item.preserve the order specification
temperature modifier
Order detail identifying hot or iced preparation.check the temperature modifier
syrup omission
Instruction not to include a named or any syrup.preserve the syrup omission
verification
Check that an item or record meets the relevant requirement.complete verification
relabeling
Changing the information attached to an item.distinguish relabeling from remaking
production error
Mistake during preparation or production.investigate a production error
entry error
Mistake when recording information.investigate an entry error
cause
Reason an event occurred, requiring evidence.avoid guessing the cause
correction request
Message identifying what must be changed.send a correction request
service recovery
Response intended to address a service failure.coordinate service recovery
acknowledgement
Clear recognition of a reported issue.give an acknowledgement
apology
Expression of regret for an error or inconvenience.offer an apology
refund
Return of money under an applicable decision or policy.request a refund review
complimentary item
Item supplied without charge under an authorized arrangement.authorize a complimentary item
queue priority
Order's position or precedence in preparation.confirm queue priority
remake status
Current stage of preparing the replacement.update remake status
checked estimate
Time estimate obtained from the relevant current source.give a checked estimate
customer-facing message
Explanation intended for the customer rather than internal shorthand.prepare a customer-facing message
final match
Verification that the finished replacement agrees with the intended order.confirm the final match

A remake, a refund, and a complimentary item are different actions. Confirm only the action actually available or authorized. Keep an unchecked wait distinct from a checked estimate and from a guaranteed completion time.

C Notice the language

Own the correction without assigning a culprit

Point to the two records and name their difference. A neutral description makes it easier to correct the order quickly. Keep an apology or acknowledgement separate from an unsupported accusation, a promised refund, or a made-up completion time.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the order
I need a correction for ticket 22.
State the ticket
The ticket says small iced latte, no syrup.
State the mismatch
The cup label says hot vanilla latte.
Protect the modifiers
Iced and no syrup both need to stay on the replacement.
Confirm collection status
The customer has not collected it.
Request the remake
Can you arrange a remake matching the ticket?
Avoid a label-only fix
Please verify the drink, not just the printed wording.
Acknowledge the issue
I see the mismatch.
Keep the cause open
We have not established how it happened.
Check timing
What is the current remake estimate?
Avoid a false promise
I have not checked the wait yet.
Separate payment
No refund decision has been made.
Avoid an extra offer
I have not been authorized to add a complimentary item.
Update the customer
We are correcting the order and checking the wait.
Verify before pickup
Match the replacement to the ticket before collection.
Close the loop
Please tell me when the checked replacement is ready.
6 language and precision notes
Says versus contains
A label says something; it does not by itself prove the drink contents.
Matching
A replacement must match all specified modifiers, not only the drink name.
Has not collected
This describes the actual stage without inventing consumption or harm.
Available versus complete
An available remake is not an already prepared replacement.
Yet
The wait has not been checked yet; this does not promise immediate service.
Separate decisions
Correction, compensation, and timing need their own factual basis.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which request preserves the order?

Answer and explanation

D. Remake ticket 22 as a small iced latte with no syrup.

The request preserves every supplied order detail and asks for a real remake.

2. Which statement should Ben avoid?

Answer and explanation

A. Your replacement will definitely be ready in one minute.

No checked wait or guaranteed completion time has been supplied.

3. What does a corrected label alone establish?

Answer and explanation

A. Corrected wording, not verified drink contents

Relabeling changes information and does not verify preparation or contents.

4. Which report avoids blame?

Answer and explanation

A. The ticket and cup label do not match.

The evidence establishes a mismatch but does not identify intent or cause.

Check explanations in the book, page 93 ↗

E Conversations

The label is not the ticket

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ben: Counter worker / Imani: Barista

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • source ticket
  • order specification
  • customer-facing message
  • mismatch
  • final match
  • remake
  • cause
  • refund
  • checked estimate
  • remake status
  1. Ben
    Hold ticket twenty-two, please. It has not been collected, but the cup label and the order do not match.
  2. Imani
    Let us compare the 1 with the label. Please give me the full drink details, including the size and any modifications.
  3. Ben
    The ticket says small iced latte with no syrup. The cup is labeled hot vanilla latte, so there are two clear differences.
  4. Imani
    I see the 2. Both the temperature description and the syrup instruction conflict with the order we need to fulfill.
  5. Ben
    The customer has not collected it. I caught it while checking the cup against the ticket, before calling the order at pickup.
  6. Imani
    Thank you. We can arrange a 3 matching the ticket. I will not treat changing the words on the label as proof the drink is correct.
  7. Ben
    Please keep both changes: iced and no syrup. The size stays small.
  8. Imani
    I will preserve the complete 4. Small iced latte, no syrup, ticket twenty-two. That is the instruction for the replacement.
  9. Ben
    Do you know the current wait? I need to tell the customer what is happening, but I have not given them a time.
  10. Imani
    I have not obtained a 5 yet. A replacement is available, but I cannot turn that into an immediate completion promise.
  11. Ben
    I will tell the customer, 'Sorry, we caught an error before pickup. We are correcting your drink and checking the wait.'
  12. Imani
    That is an accurate 6. It tells them the next step without claiming the drink is ready or the delay will be a particular length.
  13. Ben
    Should I offer a refund or an extra drink? I would rather check before making an offer that someone else has to reverse.
  14. Imani
    No 7 has been authorized in this exchange. Keep any payment or complimentary-item request within the actual service-recovery process.
  15. Ben
    All right, no promised refund or free extra. Let us sort the drink first and check how the mismatch happened afterwards.
  16. Imani
    Exactly. We have identified the difference, not the 8. We can investigate separately without making the customer wait through an argument about blame.
  17. Ben
    When the replacement is prepared, please check it against the ticket and let me know. I will use the ticket number at pickup.
  18. Imani
    Yes, the 9 matters before collection. We need the right drink and the right identifying information, not simply a newly printed label.
  19. Ben
    Thank you. For now I will say the correction is underway and the wait is still being checked, without claiming more than that.
  20. Imani
    I will update you on 10 as it changes. Until the replacement is prepared and checked, we should not describe ticket twenty-two as ready.

Answers and explanations
  1. source ticket Turn 2. The source ticket is the order record used to establish the intended drink rather than trusting the conflicting label.
  2. mismatch Turn 4. Mismatch describes the disagreement between iced and no syrup on the ticket and hot vanilla on the label.
  3. remake Turn 6. A remake means preparing a replacement to match the order, not merely changing the label on an unchecked drink.
  4. order specification Turn 8. The order specification includes all confirmed details: small, iced, latte, and no syrup.
  5. checked estimate Turn 10. A checked estimate comes from current production information; no reliable remake wait has yet been obtained.
  6. customer-facing message Turn 12. The customer-facing message explains the correction and unresolved timing in ordinary language without inventing a promise.
  7. refund Turn 14. Refund is a separate payment decision; the availability of a remake does not establish authorization to return money.
  8. cause Turn 16. Cause requires evidence beyond the observed disagreement; the exchange does not establish who or what produced the error.
  9. final match Turn 18. The final match verifies the replacement against the intended order before handover, rather than assuming a new cup is automatically correct.
  10. remake status Turn 20. Remake status reports the actual stage of the replacement; it must not convert an intended correction into completed work.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ben: Hold ticket twenty-two, please. It has not been collected, but the cup label and the order do not match.
  2. Imani: Let us compare the source ticket with the label. Please give me the full drink details, including the size and any modifications.
  3. Ben: The ticket says small iced latte with no syrup. The cup is labeled hot vanilla latte, so there are two clear differences.
  4. Imani: I see the mismatch. Both the temperature description and the syrup instruction conflict with the order we need to fulfill.
  5. Ben: The customer has not collected it. I caught it while checking the cup against the ticket, before calling the order at pickup.
  6. Imani: Thank you. We can arrange a remake matching the ticket. I will not treat changing the words on the label as proof the drink is correct.
  7. Ben: Please keep both changes: iced and no syrup. The size stays small.
  8. Imani: I will preserve the complete order specification. Small iced latte, no syrup, ticket twenty-two. That is the instruction for the replacement.
  9. Ben: Do you know the current wait? I need to tell the customer what is happening, but I have not given them a time.
  10. Imani: I have not obtained a checked estimate yet. A replacement is available, but I cannot turn that into an immediate completion promise.
  11. Ben: I will tell the customer, 'Sorry, we caught an error before pickup. We are correcting your drink and checking the wait.'
  12. Imani: That is an accurate customer-facing message. It tells them the next step without claiming the drink is ready or the delay will be a particular length.
  13. Ben: Should I offer a refund or an extra drink? I would rather check before making an offer that someone else has to reverse.
  14. Imani: No refund has been authorized in this exchange. Keep any payment or complimentary-item request within the actual service-recovery process.
  15. Ben: All right, no promised refund or free extra. Let us sort the drink first and check how the mismatch happened afterwards.
  16. Imani: Exactly. We have identified the difference, not the cause. We can investigate separately without making the customer wait through an argument about blame.
  17. Ben: When the replacement is prepared, please check it against the ticket and let me know. I will use the ticket number at pickup.
  18. Imani: Yes, the final match matters before collection. We need the right drink and the right identifying information, not simply a newly printed label.
  19. Ben: Thank you. For now I will say the correction is underway and the wait is still being checked, without claiming more than that.
  20. Imani: I will update you on remake status as it changes. Until the replacement is prepared and checked, we should not describe ticket twenty-two as ready.
Dialogue in the book, page 46 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The label contradicts the ticket

The uncollected drink on ticket 22 from the case.

  1. Customer: This cup label says hot vanilla, but my ticket is for an iced latte without syrup.
  2. Barista: You're right. Those details don't match, and I'm sorry for the mix-up here.
  3. Customer: Please don't just change the writing on the cup. I want the drink I ordered.
  4. Barista: I'll request a fresh remake using the ticket details, then check the expected wait.
  5. Customer: Is it still going to be a small, or will the replacement size change?
  6. Barista: It stays small unless you request a change. Would you still like the original specifications?
  7. Customer: Yes: small iced latte, whole milk, and no syrup added to that drink.
  8. Barista: I've repeated those details to the bar. They estimate three minutes for the replacement.
  9. Customer: Thank you. I'll wait here, and please use my ticket number when you call it.
  10. Barista: I will. That's ticket twenty-two; I'll check the label against these details at pickup.
A taste complaint needs a question

A barista clarifies a drink concern without diagnosing the preparation.

  1. Customer: This latte tastes much sweeter than I expected. I can't tell what's different.
  2. Barista: I'm sorry it isn't what you expected. Did you request the version without vanilla syrup?
  3. Customer: Yes, and the label says no vanilla. I can't tell whether that's what was made.
  4. Barista: The label shows the request, but I'll ask the bar to check the preparation.
  5. Customer: Please don't tell me I'm imagining it. I just want the less-sweet drink I ordered.
  6. Barista: I understand. I'll describe the sweetness concern and confirm your preferred order for a remake.
  7. Customer: I'd like the same milk and size, with no vanilla added to the replacement.
  8. Barista: Understood. The milk itself has its listed ingredients; I won't promise the whole drink is unsweetened.
  9. Customer: That's fine. I'm only asking you to leave out the additional vanilla syrup.
  10. Barista: I'll relay that exact change and check the remake estimate with the bar.
A complaint about texture

A customer explains that foam, not coffee strength, is the issue.

  1. Barista: You said the drink wasn't right. Is the concern its temperature, flavor, or texture?
  2. Customer: The foam is the issue. I expected less of it when I ordered this latte.
  3. Barista: Thank you for explaining. You're not asking for more coffee or a different milk, correct?
  4. Customer: Correct. I'd like the same coffee and milk, with the foam request clarified to the bar.
  5. Barista: I'll ask which remake option they can offer under our drink menu today.
  6. Customer: Please check first. I don't want to wait for a drink that repeats the same problem.
  7. Barista: The bar can remake your latte with the less-foam request noted on the ticket.
  8. Customer: That works. Is the replacement already underway, or are you waiting for my decision?
  9. Barista: I was waiting for your choice. I'll confirm it now and ask for the estimated wait.
  10. Customer: Thanks. The texture was the problem; I'm happy to keep everything else the same.

Answer and explanation

D. oat drink

Oat drink is the confirmed product choice on ticket 35.

3. Counter: "The ticket also requests ___."

Answer and explanation

A. no syrup

The syrup omission must remain intact while correcting the other details.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Lesson 05: Handling remakes and mistaken labels
Original fictional case: Ticket 22 requests a small iced latte with no syrup. Its cup label says hot vanilla latte. The customer has not collected the drink. Counter worker Ben catches the mismatch and asks barista Imani to arrange a replacement matching the ticket. A remake is available, but the current wait has not been checked. No refund, complimentary extra, or queue priority has been authorized. The conversation concerns correcting and verifying the order, not guessing how the mismatch happened or merely relabeling an unchecked drink.
Language workshop: The label is not the ticket
Communication goal: Identify an order-label mismatch, request a checked remake, and keep timing and payment decisions separate.
Language guidance: A label can be wrong even when the intended order is clear. Compare it with the source ticket. Changing the label alone does not verify what is in the cup or complete the requested remake.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I need a correction for ticket 22. / The ticket says small iced latte, no syrup. / The cup label says hot vanilla latte. / Iced and no syrup both need to stay on the replacement. / The customer has not collected it. / Can you arrange a remake matching the ticket? / Please verify the drink, not just the printed wording. / I see the mismatch. / We have not established how it happened. / What is the current remake estimate? / I have not checked the wait yet. / No refund decision has been made. / I have not been authorized to add a complimentary item. / We are correcting the order and checking the wait. / Match the replacement to the ticket before collection. / Please tell me when the checked replacement is ready.
Vocabulary: remake: New preparation intended to replace an incorrect or unsatisfactory item. / replacement: Item supplied instead of another item. / mismatch: Difference between records or items that should agree. / source ticket: Original order record used to verify the intended drink. / incorrect label: Cup information that does not match the intended order. / order specification: Complete stated details of the requested item. / temperature modifier: Order detail identifying hot or iced preparation. / syrup omission: Instruction not to include a named or any syrup. / verification: Check that an item or record meets the relevant requirement. / relabeling: Changing the information attached to an item. / production error: Mistake during preparation or production. / entry error: Mistake when recording information. / cause: Reason an event occurred, requiring evidence. / correction request: Message identifying what must be changed. / service recovery: Response intended to address a service failure. / acknowledgement: Clear recognition of a reported issue. / apology: Expression of regret for an error or inconvenience. / refund: Return of money under an applicable decision or policy. / complimentary item: Item supplied without charge under an authorized arrangement. / queue priority: Order's position or precedence in preparation. / remake status: Current stage of preparing the replacement. / checked estimate: Time estimate obtained from the relevant current source. / customer-facing message: Explanation intended for the customer rather than internal shorthand. / final match: Verification that the finished replacement agrees with the intended order.
Speaking task: Read the correction call, stressing iced and no syrup. Repeat Ben's customer message without adding a wait or compensation offer. Complete the new mismatch case and check the full replacement specification.
Partner: Ben: Counter worker / Imani: Barista
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Ticket 35 requests a large hot latte with oat drink and no syrup. The label says iced with dairy milk. A remake is available; the wait remains unchecked.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ben: When the replacement is prepared, please check it against the ticket and let me know. I will use the ticket number at pickup.
Imani: Yes, the final match matters before collection. We need the right drink and the right identifying information, not simply a newly printed label.
Ben: Thank you. For now I will say the correction is underway and the wait is still being checked, without claiming more than that.
Imani: I will update you on remake status as it changes. Until the replacement is prepared and checked, we should not describe ticket twenty-two as ready.
END REFERENCE
06Discussing coffee beans for homeBefore the beans are ground+

Your goal

Clarify the intended brewer, explain sensory wording, and avoid an irreversible choice based on an ambiguous request.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Before the beans are ground

Customer Jamie is buying a gift bag and asks for normal ground coffee. Jamie does not know the recipient brewer or whether the recipient owns a grinder. This cafe sells the same coffee whole or ground, but grinding cannot be undone. The bag describes a hazelnut flavor note; that wording describes a sensory impression, not an ingredient declaration. Barista Rafael needs to explain the distinction, ask for useful brewing information, and avoid guessing a grind setting or declaring allergen suitability from the tasting note. No purchase choice has yet been finalized.

Jamie: Gift customer / Rafael: Barista

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What information is missing?

Answer and explanation

D. The recipient's brewer and grinder availability

The customer does not know the brewing equipment or whether a grinder is available.

2. What does hazelnut mean on this bag?

Answer and explanation

C. A flavor note, not an ingredient declaration

The brief explicitly defines the wording as a sensory description.

3. What should happen before choosing a grind?

Answer and explanation

A. Clarify the intended brewing method

The brewing method is needed to choose a relevant grind rather than guessing.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A hazelnut tasting note does not establish added hazelnut, and it does not certify absence of allergens. Ingredient and handling questions require relevant product information, not an interpretation of flavor language.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
whole bean
Coffee sold as intact roasted beans rather than ground particles.choose whole bean
ground coffee
Roasted coffee reduced to particles for brewing.buy ground coffee
grind size
Size of the coffee particles after grinding.match the grind size
grind setting
Grinder adjustment used to produce a target particle size.confirm the grind setting
brewer
Device or equipment used to make coffee.identify the brewer
brewing method
Process used to extract coffee into water.clarify the brewing method
drip brewer
Device that passes water through coffee held in a filter.use a drip brewer
pour-over
Brewing method in which water is poured over coffee in a filter.prepare a pour-over
French press
Immersion brewer with a plunger and filter.identify a French press
espresso machine
Device that brews espresso using pressurized water.identify an espresso machine
cold brew
Coffee extracted using cool or cold water over an extended period.distinguish cold brew
burr grinder
Grinder that reduces beans between two abrasive grinding surfaces.use a burr grinder
blade grinder
Grinder that chops beans using rotating blades.identify a blade grinder
coarse grind
Relatively large coffee particles.request a coarse grind
fine grind
Relatively small coffee particles.request a fine grind
particle size
Dimension of individual ground-coffee pieces.compare particle size
roast level
Degree to which coffee has been roasted.describe the roast level
origin
Geographic source identified for a coffee.check the coffee origin
blend
Coffee combining more than one component lot or source.describe a blend
single origin
Coffee presented as from one defined source, whose scope should be checked.clarify single origin
tasting note
Word describing a sensory impression of flavor or aroma.explain a tasting note
aroma
Smell perceived from coffee.describe the aroma
ingredient declaration
Information stating what ingredients a product contains.check the ingredient declaration
gift recipient
Person who will receive the purchased gift.ask about the gift recipient

Normal ground is not a universal technical setting. Ask about the brewer before grinding. Whole beans avoid an immediate grind choice, but they are not automatically convenient if the recipient has no suitable grinder.

C Notice the language

Make expertise usable

Customers buying gifts may not know equipment names. Explain why the brewer matters before listing specialist terms. Offer a clear next step, such as obtaining the brewer name, while leaving the purchase choice with the customer rather than pretending one grind fits every method.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Ask about use
How will the recipient brew the coffee?
Clarify normal
Which brewing method do you mean by normal ground?
Explain the reason
The useful grind depends on the brewer.
Ask for equipment
Do you know the brewer name or type?
Offer recognizable examples
Is it a drip brewer, French press, or espresso machine?
Avoid guessing
I do not want to choose a grind from that word alone.
Explain the irreversible step
Once we grind these beans, we cannot turn them back into whole beans.
Keep the options clear
We sell this coffee whole or ground.
Ask about a grinder
Does the recipient have a grinder?
Clarify the label
Hazelnut here is a tasting note.
Separate ingredients
That is not an ingredient declaration.
Avoid an allergy claim
The flavor note does not establish allergen suitability.
Describe sensory language
It names a flavor impression someone may perceive.
Leave the choice open
We have not finalized the grind choice yet.
Offer a next step
You could check the brewer details before we grind the bag.
Confirm before action
I will wait for a clear choice before grinding.
6 language and precision notes
Ground versus grind
Ground is the product form; grind can name the particle size or the action.
For
Ground for a French press identifies intended use, not coffee origin.
Note
A tasting note describes perception rather than a list of added ingredients.
Could
A suggested next step leaves the decision with the customer.
Cannot undo
State the practical consequence before the irreversible preparation step.
Not established
Unknown equipment should remain unknown rather than becoming an assumed default.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response to normal ground is strongest?

Answer and explanation

A. What brewer will the recipient use?

The brewer supplies the missing context needed for a useful grind choice.

2. What can Rafael infer from hazelnut alone?

Answer and explanation

B. A stated sensory description, not ingredient or allergy status

Tasting language does not establish product composition, handling, or recipient equipment.

3. Why not automatically recommend whole beans?

Answer and explanation

D. The recipient may not have a suitable grinder.

Grinder availability is unknown, so convenience cannot be assumed.

4. Which statement preserves the decision stage?

Answer and explanation

C. The grind choice has not been finalized.

Neither the intended brewer nor a final grind choice is established.

Answers and explanations
  1. brewing method Turn 2. Brewing method identifies how the recipient makes coffee and helps resolve the ambiguous normal-ground request.
  2. brewer Turn 4. Brewer is the actual coffee-making equipment, whose type is still unknown.
  3. grind size Turn 6. Grind size must be chosen before grinding because reducing particles cannot be reversed into larger particles.
  4. whole bean Turn 8. Whole bean means unground roasted beans, which preserve a later grind choice but require a suitable grinder.
  5. grind setting Turn 10. Grind setting remains undecided because the recipient's equipment has not been established.
  6. tasting note Turn 12. Tasting note describes a sensory impression, not a declaration that hazelnuts were added.
  7. ingredient declaration Turn 14. An ingredient declaration states product composition, while sensory notes describe perceived flavor.
  8. allergen suitability Turn 16. Allergen suitability cannot be established from a flavor note; product and relevant handling information must be checked.
  9. brewer type Turn 18. Brewer type supplies practical equipment context for choosing a suitable grind instead of guessing from normal.
  10. purchase choice Turn 20. Purchase choice is still open; pausing the grinding does not complete a whole-bean sale.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Jamie: Could you grind this bag for a gift? Just normal ground, please. My friend makes coffee at home.
  2. Rafael: What brewing method do they use: a drip machine, a French press, or an espresso machine?
  3. Jamie: I have seen a machine in the kitchen, but I could not tell you which kind.
  4. Rafael: The brewer matters because the same grind will not suit every method equally well.
  5. Jamie: Could we start fine, then make it coarser if I discover they use something else?
  6. Rafael: We cannot make already-ground particles larger again. It is better to confirm the grind size first.
  7. Jamie: You also sell this exact coffee as beans, do you not?
  8. Rafael: Yes, it is available whole bean. Does your friend have a grinder?
  9. Jamie: I am not sure. I could message their partner without giving the gift away.
  10. Rafael: That would help. I have not chosen a grind setting or opened the bag for grinding.
  11. Jamie: While I am looking at the label, what does hazelnut mean? Is this flavored coffee with nuts added?
  12. Rafael: Here it is a tasting note, describing a flavor impression in the coffee.
  13. Jamie: Like describing a wine as fruity? It does not mean someone put that fruit into it?
  14. Rafael: That is the distinction. A sensory description is not an ingredient declaration. For ingredients, we check the actual product information.
  15. Jamie: Does that also mean the word cannot tell me whether it is suitable for someone with a nut allergy?
  16. Rafael: Correct. We cannot establish allergen suitability from that note, either for or against.
  17. Jamie: Understood. I will ask about the equipment first. Would a photograph of the machine help if they do not know its name?
  18. Rafael: It may help us identify the brewer type. A model name is useful too; we can ask again if it is unclear.
  19. Jamie: Please leave the beans as they are while I check. I have not decided whether to buy whole or ground yet.
  20. Rafael: Of course. The purchase choice is yours. We will confirm the form you want before grinding or completing the sale.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Normal ground for an unknown brewer

The gift-bag purchase from the case.

  1. Customer: Could you grind this gift bag normally? I don't know much about their coffee maker.
  2. Barista: Which brewer does the recipient use? We need that detail before choosing a grind size.
  3. Customer: I'm not sure. I only know they make coffee at home every morning.
  4. Barista: Then we can leave the beans whole while you check, since grinding can't be undone.
  5. Customer: That sounds better. Does the hazelnut note mean hazelnut flavoring has been added?
  6. Barista: It's a tasting description, not an ingredient declaration. We can check the actual product information.
  7. Customer: Please do. I don't want to describe the gift incorrectly when I hand it over.
  8. Barista: I'll show you the package information and keep any ingredient question separate from the flavor notes.
  9. Customer: Thanks. I'll buy the whole-bean bag and ask about their brewer before requesting grinding.
  10. Barista: Certainly. I'll leave the package unchanged and confirm the whole-bean item at the till.
A roast date is not a purchase date

A customer compares a bag's roast date with the purchase date and asks about its printed storage guidance. No universal freshness guarantee is established.

  1. Customer: This sticker has a roast date, and my receipt will have today's date. Which describes the coffee?
  2. Barista: The roast date identifies when it was roasted. Today's receipt date records your purchase.
  3. Customer: I thought the older date meant the shop had already sold and returned this bag.
  4. Barista: No, that date alone doesn't indicate a return. It refers to the roasting event.
  5. Customer: Where are the storage instructions on the bag?
  6. Barista: They're printed here on the back, below the label.
  7. Customer: Please also confirm this is the whole-bean version, because the shelf had ground bags nearby.
  8. Barista: The package says whole bean. We haven't ground this bag or changed its contents.
  9. Customer: That's the one I want. I'll keep the dates separate when explaining it at home.
  10. Barista: I'll ring up the whole-bean bag, and your receipt will show today's purchase date.
A description, not an ingredient promise

A barista explains a coffee's chocolate tasting note.

  1. Customer: The label says chocolate notes. Does that mean this bag contains chocolate or milk?
  2. Barista: Those words describe perceived flavor. They don't by themselves tell us the complete ingredient information.
  3. Customer: I'm asking because I need to check ingredients, not because I want a sweeter taste.
  4. Barista: Then let's consult the product information for that question rather than interpret the tasting note.
  5. Customer: Can you also ask your lead about any information the label doesn't make clear?
  6. Barista: Certainly. I'll leave that question open until they can confirm what information we have.
  7. Customer: Would choosing the bag with citrus notes avoid the need for that ingredient check?
  8. Barista: No, a different flavor description wouldn't settle it. We'd still need the relevant product details.
  9. Customer: I'll wait for those details before choosing either bag, then. Please don't grind one yet.
  10. Barista: Of course. Both bags can stay as they are while we clarify your question.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 06: Discussing coffee beans for home
Original fictional case: Customer Jamie is buying a gift bag and asks for normal ground coffee. Jamie does not know the recipient brewer or whether the recipient owns a grinder. This cafe sells the same coffee whole or ground, but grinding cannot be undone. The bag describes a hazelnut flavor note; that wording describes a sensory impression, not an ingredient declaration. Barista Rafael needs to explain the distinction, ask for useful brewing information, and avoid guessing a grind setting or declaring allergen suitability from the tasting note. No purchase choice has yet been finalized.
Language workshop: Before the beans are ground
Communication goal: Clarify the intended brewer, explain sensory wording, and avoid an irreversible choice based on an ambiguous request.
Language guidance: A hazelnut tasting note does not establish added hazelnut, and it does not certify absence of allergens. Ingredient and handling questions require relevant product information, not an interpretation of flavor language.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): How will the recipient brew the coffee? / Which brewing method do you mean by normal ground? / The useful grind depends on the brewer. / Do you know the brewer name or type? / Is it a drip brewer, French press, or espresso machine? / I do not want to choose a grind from that word alone. / Once we grind these beans, we cannot turn them back into whole beans. / We sell this coffee whole or ground. / Does the recipient have a grinder? / Hazelnut here is a tasting note. / That is not an ingredient declaration. / The flavor note does not establish allergen suitability. / It names a flavor impression someone may perceive. / We have not finalized the grind choice yet. / You could check the brewer details before we grind the bag. / I will wait for a clear choice before grinding.
Vocabulary: whole bean: Coffee sold as intact roasted beans rather than ground particles. / ground coffee: Roasted coffee reduced to particles for brewing. / grind size: Size of the coffee particles after grinding. / grind setting: Grinder adjustment used to produce a target particle size. / brewer: Device or equipment used to make coffee. / brewing method: Process used to extract coffee into water. / drip brewer: Device that passes water through coffee held in a filter. / pour-over: Brewing method in which water is poured over coffee in a filter. / French press: Immersion brewer with a plunger and filter. / espresso machine: Device that brews espresso using pressurized water. / cold brew: Coffee extracted using cool or cold water over an extended period. / burr grinder: Grinder that reduces beans between two abrasive grinding surfaces. / blade grinder: Grinder that chops beans using rotating blades. / coarse grind: Relatively large coffee particles. / fine grind: Relatively small coffee particles. / particle size: Dimension of individual ground-coffee pieces. / roast level: Degree to which coffee has been roasted. / origin: Geographic source identified for a coffee. / blend: Coffee combining more than one component lot or source. / single origin: Coffee presented as from one defined source, whose scope should be checked. / tasting note: Word describing a sensory impression of flavor or aroma. / aroma: Smell perceived from coffee. / ingredient declaration: Information stating what ingredients a product contains. / gift recipient: Person who will receive the purchased gift.
Speaking task: Read the gift-coffee conversation and Rafael's three equipment questions. Repeat the tasting-note explanation without turning it into an allergy assurance. Complete the new gift query using the supplied equipment and choice details.
Partner: Jamie: Gift customer / Rafael: Barista
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Customer Mina confirms the recipient uses a French press and wants the bag ground for it. The bag lists cocoa as a flavor note. No allergy assessment has been made.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Jamie: Understood. I will ask about the equipment first. Would a photograph of the machine help if they do not know its name?
Rafael: It may help us identify the brewer type. A model name is useful too; we can ask again if it is unclear.
Jamie: Please leave the beans as they are while I check. I have not decided whether to buy whole or ground yet.
Rafael: Of course. The purchase choice is yours. We will confirm the form you want before grinding or completing the sale.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
