07Explaining counter prices and sold-out itemsThe combo and the upcharge+

Your goal

Explain the complete comparison, retain an applicable upcharge, and offer a confirmed available item without pressure.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

The combo and the upcharge

This fictional cafe lists a latte at $4, an oat-drink upcharge at $0.50, and a pastry at $3. Its $6 combo includes a standard latte and one eligible pastry; the oat upcharge still applies. For this transaction these figures include all compulsory charges. Customer Casey wants an oat latte and pastry. The requested almond pastry is sold out, but an eligible plain pastry is available at the same listed pastry price. Casey has not accepted that substitution or paid. Barista Dana must explain $6.50 as the oat combo, compared with $7.50 separately, without charging before the choice is confirmed.

Casey: Customer / Dana: Counter barista

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the oat combo price?

Answer and explanation

D. $6.50

The $6 combo still carries the specified $0.50 oat upcharge.

2. What would the same selected items cost separately?

Answer and explanation

C. $7.50

Four dollars plus fifty cents plus three dollars equals seven dollars fifty.

3. Which pastry is available?

Answer and explanation

A. The eligible plain pastry

The brief distinguishes the sold-out almond pastry from the available plain option.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The oat upcharge applies in both comparisons. Combo: $6 + $0.50 = $6.50. Separate: $4 + $0.50 + $3 = $7.50. The saving is $1 for the same selected items.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
combo
Group of items sold together under a specified offer.explain the combo
upcharge
Extra amount added for a selected variation.apply the oat upcharge
base price
Price before specified optional additions.state the base price
standalone price
Price for an item purchased separately.compare the standalone price
eligible item
Item that qualifies for a particular offer.confirm an eligible item
included item
Item covered by the stated offer price.name the included items
optional addition
Extra item or variation selected by the customer.confirm an optional addition
price breakdown
Explanation of the components making up a price.give a price breakdown
combined price
Price for the selected items together.state the combined price
separate purchase
Purchase of items at their individual prices.compare a separate purchase
price difference
Amount by which two prices differ.calculate the price difference
saving
Reduction compared with a stated comparison price.explain the saving
sold out
No stock currently available for sale.mark the pastry sold out
available alternative
Different item that can currently be supplied.offer an available alternative
substitution
Replacement of a requested item with another.confirm a substitution
customer acceptance
Customer's explicit agreement to an offered choice.obtain customer acceptance
promotion terms
Conditions governing a special offer.check the promotion terms
compulsory charge
Amount required rather than optionally selected.include compulsory charges
displayed price
Price shown to customers.verify the displayed price
checkout total
Amount due for the confirmed transaction.confirm the checkout total
point of sale
System or place where the sale is recorded and paid.check the point of sale
payment authorization
Approval for a payment transaction.obtain payment authorization
itemized receipt
Receipt showing individual items or charges.provide an itemized receipt
stock status
Whether and how an item is available.update the stock status

An available pastry is not an accepted substitution. Confirm the actual item and price before payment. These figures include compulsory charges only because this fictional transaction explicitly says so; real menus and tax arrangements vary.

C Notice the language

An offer needs a genuine choice

A confident price explanation names what is included and what costs extra. When an item is sold out, state that directly and offer a real available alternative. Avoid making a substitution sound compulsory just because it fits a promotion.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Explain the shortage
The almond pastry is sold out.
Offer the available item
We have the plain pastry available.
Keep acceptance open
Would you like that instead?
State the base offer
The standard latte-and-pastry combo is six dollars.
Retain the upcharge
Oat drink adds fifty cents, including with the combo.
Give the combined price
That makes the oat combo six dollars fifty.
Give the separate price
Bought separately, those items are seven dollars fifty.
Explain the saving
The combo is one dollar less for those same items.
Name the eligible pastry
The plain pastry qualifies for this offer.
Avoid a forced substitute
You do not have to choose the alternative.
Clarify included charges
These stated prices include the compulsory charges here.
Check the choice
Shall I enter the oat combo with the plain pastry?
Pause payment
I will confirm the choice before taking payment.
Read the breakdown
Six dollars for the combo, plus fifty cents for oat.
Correct a misunderstanding
The combo does not remove the oat upcharge.
Keep the record clear
The receipt should match the items you actually select.
6 language and precision notes
Including with
This phrase makes clear that the surcharge survives the promotion.
Instead
Use instead only for an offered or accepted replacement, not a silent change.
Same items
A fair saving comparison keeps the selected items unchanged.
Six fifty
Common spoken form for $6.50; repeat dollars and cents if unclear.
Sold out
This describes current stock, not permanent removal from the menu.
Before payment
Confirm item choice and applicable charges before processing the transaction.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which calculation is correct?

Answer and explanation

B. $6 + $0.50 = $6.50

The oat charge is added to the combo's six-dollar base.

2. How much less is the combo than separate purchase?

Answer and explanation

D. $1.00

Seven dollars fifty minus six dollars fifty equals one dollar.

3. Which statement wrongly treats an offer as acceptance?

Answer and explanation

D. I have charged you for the plain pastry because almond is sold out.

Availability does not establish the customer's agreement to the alternative.

4. Why mention charge inclusion?

Answer and explanation

D. To keep this transaction's stated comparison complete

The brief explicitly includes compulsory charges, so the comparison can use the supplied totals.

E Conversations

The combo and the upcharge

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Casey: Customer / Dana: Counter barista

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

  • separate price
  • selection

  • sold out

  1. Casey
    Could I have an oat latte and an almond pastry? I saw a combo on the board, but I am not sure how the oat charge works.
  2. Dana
    I can explain it. First, the almond pastry is 1. We have a plain pastry available that qualifies for the combo, if you would like that instead.
  3. Casey
    Possibly. Please tell me the price before I decide, because I would like to compare the offer with buying the items separately.
  4. Dana
    The standard latte-and-pastry 2 is six dollars. Oat drink adds fifty cents, and that addition still applies when you choose the combo.
  5. Casey
    So the offer does not mean the oat drink is automatically included at no extra charge? I want to make sure I heard that correctly.
  6. Dana
    Correct. The oat 3 is fifty cents in either comparison. With it, the combo price is six dollars fifty.
  7. Casey
    And if I bought the latte with oat and the plain pastry as separate items, what would those same choices come to?
  8. Dana
    The 4 is seven dollars fifty: four dollars for the latte, fifty cents for oat drink, and three dollars for the pastry.
  9. Casey
    So it is a dollar less for the same oat latte and plain pastry. I was forgetting the oat charge in one total.
  10. Dana
    Yes, the 5 is one dollar for those same choices. Comparing the same milk option in both totals keeps the difference clear.
  11. Casey
    Does the plain pastry definitely qualify, or does the offer only apply to the almond one that is no longer available?
  12. Dana
    The plain pastry is an 6. It has the same three-dollar separate price and can be the pastry in this combo.
  13. Casey
    Thank you. Are those the complete prices for this transaction, or will another required charge appear when you ring it up?
  14. Dana
    These figures include the 7 here. The oat combo is six dollars fifty; the same items separately are seven dollars fifty.
  15. Casey
    I might just have the latte. I am not sure I want the plain pastry instead.
  16. Dana
    Of course. An available 8 is your choice, not an instruction for me to charge you. I will wait for you to confirm the actual items.
  17. Casey
    I appreciate that. Could you repeat the combo breakdown once more, without the separate prices, so I can hear the two parts clearly?
  18. Dana
    The 9 is six dollars for the combo plus fifty cents for oat drink. That is six dollars fifty with the plain pastry.
  19. Casey
    Thanks. Let me look at the plain pastry before I decide. I have not chosen it yet.
  20. Dana
    Certainly. We will confirm your 10 before payment. I have explained the options, but I will not enter a substitution as though you had already agreed.

Answers and explanations
  1. sold out Turn 2. Sold out describes the requested pastry's current unavailability, which must be disclosed before offering another item.
  2. combo Turn 4. Combo names the six-dollar package containing the standard latte and one eligible pastry.
  3. upcharge Turn 6. Upcharge is the additional fifty cents that applies both to the combo and to the separately purchased latte.
  4. separate price Turn 8. The separate price adds the four-dollar latte, fifty-cent oat variation, and three-dollar pastry, totaling seven dollars fifty.
  5. saving Turn 10. Saving is the one-dollar difference between the same selected items bought as a combo or separately.
  6. eligible item Turn 12. Eligible item means the available plain pastry qualifies for the stated combo rather than being excluded from the promotion.
  7. compulsory charges Turn 14. Compulsory charges are explicitly included in this fictional transaction, making the stated comparison complete for these choices.
  8. alternative Turn 16. Alternative names a possible replacement; it is not an accepted selection until the customer chooses it.
  9. price breakdown Turn 18. Price breakdown separates the six-dollar combo base from the fifty-cent oat addition, producing the stated six-fifty total.
  10. selection Turn 20. Selection is the customer's actual choice, still needed before treating the proposed plain-pastry combo as the agreed transaction.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The upcharge remains in the combo

The fictional combo prices from the case.

  1. Customer: Your sign says six dollars for a latte and pastry. Does that include oat drink?
  2. Barista: The combo includes the standard latte; the fifty-cent oat upcharge still applies to that choice.
  3. Customer: So my oat latte with this pastry would be six fifty altogether for those items?
  4. Barista: Yes. The same selected items bought separately would total seven fifty on our menu.
  5. Customer: Then the combo saves a dollar, not a dollar and fifty cents, correct?
  6. Barista: Correct. Both versions include the same oat change, so the saving is one dollar.
  7. Customer: I'll choose the combo. Please keep the drink size that qualifies for this offer.
  8. Barista: Certainly. That's the listed small latte size; your oat choice stays on the ticket.
  9. Customer: Thank you for explaining the upcharge before payment. I didn't see the small-print condition.
  10. Barista: I'll enter the six-fifty combo and show you the item details before you pay.
A sold-out pastry is a choice point

A counter worker asks before substituting a pastry.

  1. Counter worker: The cinnamon pastry has just sold out. We still have plain pastries at the same price.
  2. Customer: I ordered cinnamon because of the flavor. Please don't just put a plain one in the bag.
  3. Counter worker: Of course. Would you like the plain alternative, another listed item, or no pastry today?
  4. Customer: No pastry, please. Can I keep the coffee order without the combo?
  5. Counter worker: Yes. I'll change it to the standalone coffee price and explain the revised total.
  6. Customer: Does that mean the coffee still costs its normal four dollars rather than the combo price?
  7. Counter worker: Correct. The standard coffee alone is four dollars, with no pastry included in that amount.
  8. Customer: That works. Please confirm the pastry has been removed before I make the payment.
  9. Counter worker: The ticket now shows one standard coffee only. There's no pastry or combo charge.
  10. Customer: Thank you. I wanted to choose, even though the alternative pastry cost the same.
A reward has a specific condition

A fictional reward covers a standard filter coffee, not all drinks.

  1. Customer: My account has a loyalty reward for a free drink. Does it cover a large specialty latte?
  2. Counter worker: The displayed reward here covers a standard filter coffee; it doesn't list the specialty latte.
  3. Customer: I thought free drink meant any drink. Can you show me the condition on the offer?
  4. Counter worker: Certainly. This line specifies the filter coffee and eligible size, rather than the whole menu.
  5. Customer: Could I pay only the difference for the latte, or is that not stated?
  6. Counter worker: That option isn't stated here. I'll ask the lead instead of inventing an exchange value.
  7. Customer: Please do. Don't use the reward while we're checking, because I may save it.
  8. Counter worker: Understood. It remains unused while the lead confirms whether any alternative arrangement is available.
  9. Customer: If there isn't one, I'll buy the latte normally and keep the reward for later.
  10. Counter worker: I'll confirm the lead's answer with you before choosing either option at the till.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The combo and the upcharge

Calculate another cafe offer

A different cafe lists a $7 standard combo and a $0.75 oat upcharge that still applies. The same items separately total $9.25. All compulsory charges are included. The muffin is available but not yet accepted.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Counter: "The oat combo total is ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

A. 7.75

Seven dollars plus seventy-five cents gives seven dollars seventy-five.

2. Customer: "The separate total is ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

A. 9.25

Nine dollars twenty-five is the separate total supplied in the brief.

3. Counter: "The combo saves ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

C. 1.50

Nine dollars twenty-five minus seven dollars seventy-five equals one dollar fifty.

4. Counter: "The muffin substitution is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

B. accepted

Availability does not show agreement, and acceptance remains pending here.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Conversation explanations in the book, page 98 ↗

Lesson 07: Explaining counter prices and sold-out items
Original fictional case: This fictional cafe lists a latte at $4, an oat-drink upcharge at $0.50, and a pastry at $3. Its $6 combo includes a standard latte and one eligible pastry; the oat upcharge still applies. For this transaction these figures include all compulsory charges. Customer Casey wants an oat latte and pastry. The requested almond pastry is sold out, but an eligible plain pastry is available at the same listed pastry price. Casey has not accepted that substitution or paid. Barista Dana must explain $6.50 as the oat combo, compared with $7.50 separately, without charging before the choice is confirmed.
Language workshop: The combo and the upcharge
Communication goal: Explain the complete comparison, retain an applicable upcharge, and offer a confirmed available item without pressure.
Language guidance: The oat upcharge applies in both comparisons. Combo: $6 + $0.50 = $6.50. Separate: $4 + $0.50 + $3 = $7.50. The saving is $1 for the same selected items.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The almond pastry is sold out. / We have the plain pastry available. / Would you like that instead? / The standard latte-and-pastry combo is six dollars. / Oat drink adds fifty cents, including with the combo. / That makes the oat combo six dollars fifty. / Bought separately, those items are seven dollars fifty. / The combo is one dollar less for those same items. / The plain pastry qualifies for this offer. / You do not have to choose the alternative. / These stated prices include the compulsory charges here. / Shall I enter the oat combo with the plain pastry? / I will confirm the choice before taking payment. / Six dollars for the combo, plus fifty cents for oat. / The combo does not remove the oat upcharge. / The receipt should match the items you actually select.
Vocabulary: combo: Group of items sold together under a specified offer. / upcharge: Extra amount added for a selected variation. / base price: Price before specified optional additions. / standalone price: Price for an item purchased separately. / eligible item: Item that qualifies for a particular offer. / included item: Item covered by the stated offer price. / optional addition: Extra item or variation selected by the customer. / price breakdown: Explanation of the components making up a price. / combined price: Price for the selected items together. / separate purchase: Purchase of items at their individual prices. / price difference: Amount by which two prices differ. / saving: Reduction compared with a stated comparison price. / sold out: No stock currently available for sale. / available alternative: Different item that can currently be supplied. / substitution: Replacement of a requested item with another. / customer acceptance: Customer's explicit agreement to an offered choice. / promotion terms: Conditions governing a special offer. / compulsory charge: Amount required rather than optionally selected. / displayed price: Price shown to customers. / checkout total: Amount due for the confirmed transaction. / point of sale: System or place where the sale is recorded and paid. / payment authorization: Approval for a payment transaction. / itemized receipt: Receipt showing individual items or charges. / stock status: Whether and how an item is available.
Speaking task: Read the two prices aloud, keeping the oat charge in both comparisons. Repeat six plus fifty cents and four plus fifty cents plus three. Complete the transfer and verify that both totals compare the same items.
Partner: Casey: Customer / Dana: Counter barista
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A different cafe lists a $7 standard combo and a $0.75 oat upcharge that still applies. The same items separately total $9.25. All compulsory charges are included. The muffin is available but not yet accepted.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Casey: I appreciate that. Could you repeat the combo breakdown once more, without the separate prices, so I can hear the two parts clearly?
Dana: The price breakdown is six dollars for the combo plus fifty cents for oat drink. That is six dollars fifty with the plain pastry.
Casey: Thanks. Let me look at the plain pastry before I decide. I have not chosen it yet.
Dana: Certainly. We will confirm your selection before payment. I have explained the options, but I will not enter a substitution as though you had already agreed.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

08Handing over the bar and tillOne order, three unfinished items+

Your goal

Transfer drink, stock, and cash-check responsibilities while preserving the difference between counted, pending, and signed off.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

One order, three unfinished items

At 2:00, outgoing barista Ellis briefs incoming worker Noor. Ticket 108 contains coffee and tea: the coffee is complete, but the tea is pending. Large takeaway lids are unavailable; small lids remain. Ellis has counted the till, but lead Mara has not completed reconciliation. Noor accepts the tea follow-up and will check replenishment of the correct large lids. Mara retains the till review. No tea completion, large-lid delivery, approved substitute lid, or cash sign-off is established. The handover must keep these separate tasks and stages visible.

Ellis: Outgoing barista / Noor: Incoming cafe worker

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What remains on ticket 108?

Answer and explanation

C. The tea

The coffee is complete but the tea is explicitly still pending.

2. Which lids are unavailable?

Answer and explanation

A. Large takeaway lids

The shortage is specific to large takeaway lids, while small lids remain.

3. What is the till status?

Answer and explanation

D. Counted, with Mara's reconciliation pending

A count is complete, but the separate reconciliation has not been completed.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Coffee complete does not mean ticket complete. Till counted does not mean till reconciled. A handover should preserve each item and stage so the incoming worker does not inherit a misleading all-done summary.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
shift handover
Transfer of current work information between outgoing and incoming staff.conduct a shift handover
outgoing worker
Person transferring work at the end of a shift or assignment.brief the outgoing worker
incoming worker
Person receiving responsibility for continuing work.brief the incoming worker
open ticket
Order record with unfinished items or unresolved work.identify an open ticket
item-level status
State of each individual item within an order.report item-level status
remaining item
Item still needing completion within a known order.name the remaining item
tea follow-up
Responsibility for checking and completing the pending tea through the actual process.accept the tea follow-up
takeaway lid
Cover designed for the specified takeaway cup.check the takeaway lid
lid size
Size specification relevant to the matching cup.confirm the lid size
stockout
Situation in which a required stock item is unavailable.report a stockout
replenishment
Restoration of stock through the relevant supply process.check replenishment
compatible item
Item verified to fit and function for the intended use.verify a compatible item
stock follow-up
Task of checking availability or replenishment.assign stock follow-up
delivery estimate
Approximate expected arrival time for a supply item.verify a delivery estimate
till
Cash drawer or register arrangement used for transactions.count the till
cash count
Physical tally of cash held at a stated point.record the cash count
reconciliation
Comparison of records and amounts to identify and resolve differences.complete reconciliation
expected balance
Amount records indicate should be present.compare the expected balance
actual balance
Amount found by the relevant count or measurement.record the actual balance
variance
Difference between compared figures.investigate a variance
review owner
Person responsible for the pending review.name the review owner
sign-off
Required confirmation that a review or process is complete.obtain sign-off
pending review
Review not yet completed.retain pending-review status
handover read-back
Repetition of transferred tasks and states for confirmation.give a handover read-back

Small lids remaining do not prove they fit large cups. Report the exact shortage and request the correct replenishment or an approved compatible option through the actual process. Do not promise an arrival time without a checked estimate.

C Notice the language

Receiving a task is not completing it

A handover benefits from a short read-back with the order number, outstanding item, and next owner. A colleague can confidently accept responsibility while saying a check remains pending. Do not make the end of a shift sound like every task has ended.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Open the handover
I have three items to hand over at two o'clock.
Identify the open order
Ticket 108 still needs its tea.
Separate completion
The coffee is complete; the tea is pending.
Assign the follow-up
Can you take responsibility for the tea follow-up?
Accept responsibility
Yes, I will take that follow-up.
Name the shortage
Large takeaway lids are unavailable.
Keep remaining stock distinct
Small lids remain, but that does not establish compatibility.
Request replenishment
Please check the correct large-lid stock.
Avoid an arrival promise
There is no checked delivery estimate.
State the cash stage
The till has been counted.
Preserve the pending stage
Mara's reconciliation is still pending.
Name the owner
Mara retains the till review.
Avoid false sign-off
I am not reporting the till as signed off.
Read back the tasks
Tea follow-up and large-lid check are with me.
Separate acceptance
I have accepted the work; I have not completed it yet.
Close the handover
Update the records with the actual results as the tasks finish.
6 language and precision notes
Item versus order
One finished item does not establish completion of the full ticket.
Count versus reconciliation
A count produces an amount; reconciliation compares it with relevant records.
Still pending
This preserves unfinished work through a change of staff.
With me
Common shorthand for responsibility, not proof of completed action.
Correct size
State the needed specification rather than assuming another size works.
Signed off
Use only when the required completion or approval has actually occurred.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which handover is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. Ticket 108: coffee complete, tea pending; large lids unavailable; till counted, review pending.

The complete report preserves each task and its distinct current stage.

2. What does Noor's acceptance establish?

Answer and explanation

C. Responsibility for the tea and stock follow-ups

Accepting a task transfers responsibility but does not perform the task.

3. Which stock claim is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

A. Small lids are approved substitutes for the large cups.

Remaining stock alone does not establish fit, suitability, or substitution approval.

4. Who owns the pending till review?

Answer and explanation

C. Mara

The brief explicitly leaves the reconciliation review with lead Mara.

Check explanations in the book, page 99 ↗

E Conversations

One order, three unfinished items

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ellis: Outgoing barista / Noor: Incoming cafe worker

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • handover read-back
  • review owner
  • item-level status
  • sign-off
  • reconciliation
  • shift handover
  • tea follow-up
  • stock follow-up
  • cash count
  • stockout
  1. Ellis
    Before I finish, can we run through ticket one hundred eight, the large lids, and the till?
  2. Noor
    I am ready for the 1. Please take them one at a time so I can keep the customer order separate from stock and cash checks.
  3. Ellis
    Ticket one hundred eight has two drinks. The coffee is complete, but the tea is pending. It is not a fully completed order.
  4. Noor
    I will record the 2 as coffee complete, tea pending. I will not mark the entire ticket complete just because one drink is finished.
  5. Ellis
    Can you pick up that tea from here? The order details are on the ticket.
  6. Noor
    Yes, I accept the 3. That is my next responsibility on ticket one hundred eight, not a claim that I have already made the drink.
  7. Ellis
    Next, large takeaway lids are unavailable. We still have small lids, but I have not established an approved alternative for the large cups.
  8. Noor
    Understood. The 4 is for large lids. I will not assume the remaining small ones are compatible with a different cup size.
  9. Ellis
    Please check the correct large lids. I do not have a delivery estimate yet.
  10. Noor
    I will take the 5 and report what I actually find. The need is clear, but the arrival time and supply outcome remain unconfirmed.
  11. Ellis
    Finally, I have counted the till. Mara has not completed reconciliation, so the count is available but the review is still pending.
  12. Noor
    The 6 is complete, while reconciliation is not. I will not turn counted into signed off when I update the next person.
  13. Ellis
    Right. We do not have a confirmed shortage or overage. Mara still needs to compare the count with the records.
  14. Noor
    I will leave the 7 with Mara. There is no reason to invent a difference between actual and expected cash from a pending check.
  15. Ellis
    Thank you. Could you repeat who owns each unfinished part? I want to make sure none of the three items disappears when I leave.
  16. Noor
    My 8 is: I take the tea follow-up and correct large-lid stock check. Mara retains the till review, which remains pending.
  17. Ellis
    That is right. Please keep the results separate when they come in. A lid update does not close the tea order or the cash review.
  18. Noor
    Agreed. The 9 for the till remains Mara. I will record each actual outcome against its own task rather than give one blanket completion message.
  19. Ellis
    Thanks. Tea and lids with you; till review with Mara. That is everything I needed to hand over.
  20. Noor
    Yes. The handover is clear, but the till has no 10 yet. I have accepted the tea and stock tasks and will update their real status as I carry them out.

Answers and explanations
  1. shift handover Turn 2. Shift handover transfers current tasks and information; it does not imply that every outgoing task is finished.
  2. item-level status Turn 4. Item-level status distinguishes the completed coffee from the pending tea within the same ticket.
  3. tea follow-up Turn 6. Tea follow-up is the specific outstanding responsibility Noor accepts; accepting it does not establish the tea is ready.
  4. stockout Turn 8. Stockout concerns the specified large lids, not every lid or a confirmed suitable substitute.
  5. stock follow-up Turn 10. Stock follow-up assigns checking of the correct replenishment; no delivery or arrival estimate is yet confirmed.
  6. cash count Turn 12. Cash count is the completed tally, which must remain distinct from the uncompleted reconciliation against relevant records.
  7. reconciliation Turn 14. Reconciliation is the comparison and review that Mara has not completed; the handover supplies no resulting variance.
  8. handover read-back Turn 16. The handover read-back confirms both responsibility and unfinished status, reducing the chance that an open task is lost.
  9. review owner Turn 18. Review owner identifies Mara as responsible for the cash review; Noor's other accepted tasks do not transfer that review automatically.
  10. sign-off Turn 20. Sign-off is the required completed confirmation, which cannot be inferred from a cash count or a successful handover.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ellis: Before I finish, can we run through ticket one hundred eight, the large lids, and the till?
  2. Noor: I am ready for the shift handover. Please take them one at a time so I can keep the customer order separate from stock and cash checks.
  3. Ellis: Ticket one hundred eight has two drinks. The coffee is complete, but the tea is pending. It is not a fully completed order.
  4. Noor: I will record the item-level status as coffee complete, tea pending. I will not mark the entire ticket complete just because one drink is finished.
  5. Ellis: Can you pick up that tea from here? The order details are on the ticket.
  6. Noor: Yes, I accept the tea follow-up. That is my next responsibility on ticket one hundred eight, not a claim that I have already made the drink.
  7. Ellis: Next, large takeaway lids are unavailable. We still have small lids, but I have not established an approved alternative for the large cups.
  8. Noor: Understood. The stockout is for large lids. I will not assume the remaining small ones are compatible with a different cup size.
  9. Ellis: Please check the correct large lids. I do not have a delivery estimate yet.
  10. Noor: I will take the stock follow-up and report what I actually find. The need is clear, but the arrival time and supply outcome remain unconfirmed.
  11. Ellis: Finally, I have counted the till. Mara has not completed reconciliation, so the count is available but the review is still pending.
  12. Noor: The cash count is complete, while reconciliation is not. I will not turn counted into signed off when I update the next person.
  13. Ellis: Right. We do not have a confirmed shortage or overage. Mara still needs to compare the count with the records.
  14. Noor: I will leave the reconciliation with Mara. There is no reason to invent a difference between actual and expected cash from a pending check.
  15. Ellis: Thank you. Could you repeat who owns each unfinished part? I want to make sure none of the three items disappears when I leave.
  16. Noor: My handover read-back is: I take the tea follow-up and correct large-lid stock check. Mara retains the till review, which remains pending.
  17. Ellis: That is right. Please keep the results separate when they come in. A lid update does not close the tea order or the cash review.
  18. Noor: Agreed. The review owner for the till remains Mara. I will record each actual outcome against its own task rather than give one blanket completion message.
  19. Ellis: Thanks. Tea and lids with you; till review with Mara. That is everything I needed to hand over.
  20. Noor: Yes. The handover is clear, but the till has no sign-off yet. I have accepted the tea and stock tasks and will update their real status as I carry them out.
Dialogue in the book, page 73 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

One tea, one stockout, one review

The 2:00 handover from the case.

  1. Outgoing worker: Ticket one-oh-eight has its coffee, but the tea is still waiting to be prepared.
  2. Incoming worker: I'll take the tea. Is that the only unfinished drink on the current order queue?
  3. Outgoing worker: Yes. Large takeaway lids are also out, although small lids are still available here.
  4. Incoming worker: I'll ask the lead about the large-lid stockout before offering a different packaging arrangement.
  5. Outgoing worker: Thanks. I've counted the till, but the reconciliation with the sales record is pending.
  6. Incoming worker: Are you keeping that review, or do you need me to take it over?
  7. Outgoing worker: I'll stay with it until the lead accepts the follow-up. You have the tea and lid query.
  8. Incoming worker: Understood. I won't call the till signed off simply because the count has been completed.
  9. Outgoing worker: Please let the customer know the tea is outstanding, so they don't leave with only coffee.
  10. Incoming worker: I will. I'll check their full order at pickup and ask about the lids next.
A float is not today's takings

A new counter worker clarifies the till count.

  1. Counter worker: The drawer count includes the cash float. Does the whole amount represent today's cash sales?
  2. Lead: No. The float is starting change cash, so keep it distinct from the transaction record.
  3. Counter worker: I haven't done the reconciliation yet. I was about to report the whole drawer as takings.
  4. Lead: Please describe it as the counted drawer amount until we've compared the appropriate records.
  5. Counter worker: There's also a difference between my count and the figure on this summary.
  6. Lead: Report the two figures without deciding whether it means a missed sale or another issue.
  7. Counter worker: I'll bring you the count and the summary, with the cause of the difference unconfirmed.
  8. Lead: Good. Don't assign blame or invent an adjustment to make the numbers appear to match.
  9. Counter worker: Will you take the reconciliation review while I return to the counter orders?
  10. Lead: Yes, I've accepted that review. I'll ask you for any clarification about your count.
Which cup size is actually missing?

A barista clarifies a supply shortage before updating the counter.

  1. Barista: You said we're out of cups. Which size and service type does that affect?
  2. Counter colleague: Large takeaway cups only. The small takeaway cups and dine-in mugs are still stocked.
  3. Barista: Thanks. I'll tell the counter the exact size rather than describe every drink as unavailable.
  4. Counter colleague: Please ask the lead about the available service choices before promising a substitute container.
  5. Barista: I will. A customer may prefer a different size, but we should ask them first.
  6. Counter colleague: Exactly. Their choice could change the order and price, so don't silently swap the size.
  7. Barista: I'll check the permitted options and explain them before the customer decides whether to order.
  8. Counter colleague: Meanwhile, I'll count the small cups so our update includes the stock we actually have.
  9. Barista: Good. Please report that count when it's complete, not an estimate from looking at the sleeve.
  10. Counter colleague: I'll do that. The confirmed shortage remains large takeaway cups, with the small-cup count pending.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

One order, three unfinished items
  1. Read the handover with a pause between order, stock, and cash.
  2. Repeat Noor's responsibilities separately from Mara's pending review.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Transfer a different afternoon handover

Ticket 205 has tea complete and cocoa pending. Medium lids are unavailable. Incoming worker Jules accepts the cocoa and stock follow-ups. Lead Ben retains the till reconciliation, which is still pending.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Incoming: "The remaining drink is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. cocoa

Cocoa is pending, while the tea is already complete.

2. Outgoing: "The missing lid size is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. medium

Medium is the specific shortage supplied in this new handover.

3. Incoming: "The till review remains with ___."

Answer and explanation

A. Ben

Ben is explicitly identified as the owner of reconciliation.

4. Outgoing: "The till review is still ___."

Answer and explanation

A. pending

No completed reconciliation or sign-off is established in this handover.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Lesson 08: Handing over the bar and till
Original fictional case: At 2:00, outgoing barista Ellis briefs incoming worker Noor. Ticket 108 contains coffee and tea: the coffee is complete, but the tea is pending. Large takeaway lids are unavailable; small lids remain. Ellis has counted the till, but lead Mara has not completed reconciliation. Noor accepts the tea follow-up and will check replenishment of the correct large lids. Mara retains the till review. No tea completion, large-lid delivery, approved substitute lid, or cash sign-off is established. The handover must keep these separate tasks and stages visible.
Language workshop: One order, three unfinished items
Communication goal: Transfer drink, stock, and cash-check responsibilities while preserving the difference between counted, pending, and signed off.
Language guidance: Coffee complete does not mean ticket complete. Till counted does not mean till reconciled. A handover should preserve each item and stage so the incoming worker does not inherit a misleading all-done summary.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I have three items to hand over at two o'clock. / Ticket 108 still needs its tea. / The coffee is complete; the tea is pending. / Can you take responsibility for the tea follow-up? / Yes, I will take that follow-up. / Large takeaway lids are unavailable. / Small lids remain, but that does not establish compatibility. / Please check the correct large-lid stock. / There is no checked delivery estimate. / The till has been counted. / Mara's reconciliation is still pending. / Mara retains the till review. / I am not reporting the till as signed off. / Tea follow-up and large-lid check are with me. / I have accepted the work; I have not completed it yet. / Update the records with the actual results as the tasks finish.
Vocabulary: shift handover: Transfer of current work information between outgoing and incoming staff. / outgoing worker: Person transferring work at the end of a shift or assignment. / incoming worker: Person receiving responsibility for continuing work. / open ticket: Order record with unfinished items or unresolved work. / item-level status: State of each individual item within an order. / remaining item: Item still needing completion within a known order. / tea follow-up: Responsibility for checking and completing the pending tea through the actual process. / takeaway lid: Cover designed for the specified takeaway cup. / lid size: Size specification relevant to the matching cup. / stockout: Situation in which a required stock item is unavailable. / replenishment: Restoration of stock through the relevant supply process. / compatible item: Item verified to fit and function for the intended use. / stock follow-up: Task of checking availability or replenishment. / delivery estimate: Approximate expected arrival time for a supply item. / till: Cash drawer or register arrangement used for transactions. / cash count: Physical tally of cash held at a stated point. / reconciliation: Comparison of records and amounts to identify and resolve differences. / expected balance: Amount records indicate should be present. / actual balance: Amount found by the relevant count or measurement. / variance: Difference between compared figures. / review owner: Person responsible for the pending review. / sign-off: Required confirmation that a review or process is complete. / pending review: Review not yet completed. / handover read-back: Repetition of transferred tasks and states for confirmation.
Speaking task: Read the handover with a pause between order, stock, and cash. Repeat Noor's responsibilities separately from Mara's pending review. Complete the new handover without converting accepted work into completed work.
Partner: Ellis: Outgoing barista / Noor: Incoming cafe worker
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Ticket 205 has tea complete and cocoa pending. Medium lids are unavailable. Incoming worker Jules accepts the cocoa and stock follow-ups. Lead Ben retains the till reconciliation, which is still pending.

Ellis: Thanks. Tea and lids with you; till review with Mara. That is everything I needed to hand over.
Noor: Yes. The handover is clear, but the till has no sign-off yet. I have accepted the tea and stock tasks and will update their real status as I carry them out.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.

3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

More situations, different skills

Three additional workplace scenarios

Read the shot, not just the timer

Distinguish dose, beverage yield, ratio, time, and sensory observations during a recipe check.

Fictional bar check by trained staff: the house target is 18 g dry coffee to 36 g espresso. Recorded shot A is 18 g in, 36 g out, 28 seconds; B is 18 g in, 45 g out, also 28 seconds. The time uses the same starting point. These are records to discuss, not equipment-operation instructions.

Imani: Barista / Leo: Coffee lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • brew time
  • dose
  • beverage yield
  • one-to-two ratio
  • sensory observation
  • controlled comparison
  1. Imani
    Both shots took twenty-eight seconds, so I nearly marked both as matching the recipe. Then I noticed the second cup weighed more.
  2. Leo
    Start with the 1. We used eighteen grams of dry coffee in each. What came out into the cups?
  3. Imani
    Thirty-six grams for A and forty-five for B. Those are the drink weights after excluding the cups, not water added to the machine.
  4. Leo
    Right. That is the 2. The common time does not make those two outputs equal.
  5. Imani
    For A, thirty-six divided by eighteen is two. I would describe it as one part dry coffee to two parts espresso by mass.
  6. Leo
    Exactly: a 3, written one colon two. That matches the stated mass target for this house recipe.
  7. Imani
    For B, forty-five divided by eighteen is two point five. So B is one to two point five, even though the timer shows the same value.
  8. Leo
    Yes. Keep 4 beside the input and output figures. It gives us another observation, not a substitute for weighing.
  9. Imani
    I described B as sharper when we tasted it. Should I write under-extracted in the result column because of that?
  10. Leo
    Write the 5 first. That describes what you perceived without pretending we measured extraction percentage or proved the cause.
  11. Imani
    We have not measured dissolved solids for either shot, so I should not invent an extraction percentage from the ratio.
  12. Leo
    Correct. The mass ratio and extraction percentage answer different questions. A larger drink weight does not tell us the complete sensory result either.
  13. Imani
    I also need to make clear when the timer starts. Otherwise another barista could record a different time for the same run.
  14. Leo
    Yes. Use the agreed starting point and record the actual coffee, recipe, and settings. A useful comparison needs consistent definitions.
  15. Imani
    For the next check, should we change the dose, grind, and output target together?
  16. Leo
    Not for a 6. We will agree one planned change, keep the other targets consistent, and follow the approved equipment procedure.
  17. Imani
    Then I will retain both original records. I will not edit B down to thirty-six just because that was the target.
  18. Leo
    Exactly. A target is what we intended; the recorded output is what happened. Both are useful when we compare the next shot.
  19. Imani
    My handover: A matches eighteen in and thirty-six out; B has the same dose and time but forty-five out. My taste description stays separate.
  20. Leo
    That is clear. We can now choose the next approved check from the evidence rather than from the timer alone.

Answers and explanations
  1. dose Turn 2. Dose is the dry ground coffee input, 18 g in each recorded shot, not the beverage collected.
  2. beverage yield Turn 4. Beverage yield is the mass of espresso collected: 36 g for A and 45 g for B, excluding the cup.
  3. one-to-two ratio Turn 6. 18 g dry coffee to 36 g beverage simplifies to 1:2; it is not a percentage extraction measurement.
  4. brew time Turn 8. Brew time is the elapsed measurement, here 28 seconds from the same starting point; it does not by itself specify dose or output.
  5. sensory observation Turn 10. Sharper is the barista's sensory observation; it does not alone prove a measured extraction percentage or a single cause.
  6. controlled comparison Turn 16. Changing one planned factor while holding the others consistent makes the resulting comparison easier to interpret; changing everything obscures the cause.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Imani: Both shots took twenty-eight seconds, so I nearly marked both as matching the recipe. Then I noticed the second cup weighed more.
  2. Leo: Start with the dose. We used eighteen grams of dry coffee in each. What came out into the cups?
  3. Imani: Thirty-six grams for A and forty-five for B. Those are the drink weights after excluding the cups, not water added to the machine.
  4. Leo: Right. That is the beverage yield. The common time does not make those two outputs equal.
  5. Imani: For A, thirty-six divided by eighteen is two. I would describe it as one part dry coffee to two parts espresso by mass.
  6. Leo: Exactly: a one-to-two ratio, written one colon two. That matches the stated mass target for this house recipe.
  7. Imani: For B, forty-five divided by eighteen is two point five. So B is one to two point five, even though the timer shows the same value.
  8. Leo: Yes. Keep brew time beside the input and output figures. It gives us another observation, not a substitute for weighing.
  9. Imani: I described B as sharper when we tasted it. Should I write under-extracted in the result column because of that?
  10. Leo: Write the sensory observation first. That describes what you perceived without pretending we measured extraction percentage or proved the cause.
  11. Imani: We have not measured dissolved solids for either shot, so I should not invent an extraction percentage from the ratio.
  12. Leo: Correct. The mass ratio and extraction percentage answer different questions. A larger drink weight does not tell us the complete sensory result either.
  13. Imani: I also need to make clear when the timer starts. Otherwise another barista could record a different time for the same run.
  14. Leo: Yes. Use the agreed starting point and record the actual coffee, recipe, and settings. A useful comparison needs consistent definitions.
  15. Imani: For the next check, should we change the dose, grind, and output target together?
  16. Leo: Not for a controlled comparison. We will agree one planned change, keep the other targets consistent, and follow the approved equipment procedure.
  17. Imani: Then I will retain both original records. I will not edit B down to thirty-six just because that was the target.
  18. Leo: Exactly. A target is what we intended; the recorded output is what happened. Both are useful when we compare the next shot.
  19. Imani: My handover: A matches eighteen in and thirty-six out; B has the same dose and time but forty-five out. My taste description stays separate.
  20. Leo: That is clear. We can now choose the next approved check from the evidence rather than from the timer alone.
Separate output from elapsed time

Recorded shots C and D both use 20 g dry coffee. C produces 40 g espresso in 27 seconds; D produces 50 g in 27 seconds. Compare dose-to-beverage mass ratios. No dissolved-solids reading is supplied.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Barista: The dry-coffee dose is ___ grams in both shots.

Answer and explanation

D. 20

The dose is the input coffee mass, not the larger beverage weight.

2. Lead: C has a dose-to-beverage ratio of ___ .

Answer and explanation

C. 1:2

Forty grams beverage divided by twenty grams dry coffee equals two.

3. Barista: D has a dose-to-beverage ratio of ___ .

Answer and explanation

C. 1:2.5

Fifty divided by twenty equals 2.5, despite the same elapsed time.

4. Lead: The extraction percentage remains ___ .

Answer and explanation

C. unmeasured

The supplied mass ratios and times do not establish a dissolved-solids measurement or extraction percentage.

Professional context: La Marzocco: dose, beverage yield, and brew time

Strength is not extraction

Explain concentration and a practical beverage-based extraction estimate without confusing them with taste or caffeine.

Fictional paper-filtered brew: 30 g dry coffee, 550 g brew-water input, 500 g collected beverage, measured 1.35% coffee TDS. Use the simplified beverage-based estimate EY% = beverage mass x TDS% / dry dose. A later arithmetic example adds 100 g water after brewing, with no coffee-solids loss and negligible added solids. This is a calculation exercise, not a universal recipe.

Maya: Barista / Noor: Coffee lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • beverage mass
  • dilution
  • extraction yield
  • dry dose
  • concentration
  • Total dissolved solids
  1. Maya
    The brew sheet has five hundred fifty grams of water and five hundred grams of coffee collected. Which figure belongs in this extraction calculation?
  2. Noor
    Use the collected 1: five hundred grams. The water input is a different measurement, and some liquid remains with the grounds and filter.
  3. Maya
    The refractometer result is one point three five percent TDS. That means concentration, not one point three five percent of the grounds extracted?
  4. Noor
    Correct. 2 describes the coffee concentration in the beverage for this reading.
  5. Maya
    So I multiply five hundred by zero point zero one three five. That gives six point seven five grams of dissolved coffee material in the collected drink.
  6. Noor
    Yes, using the supplied measurement. Then divide that by the 3 of thirty grams and multiply by one hundred.
  7. Maya
    That gives twenty-two point five percent. I should call it the beverage-based estimate in this exercise, not a perfect measurement of everything removed from the grounds.
  8. Noor
    Right. That practical 4 estimate excludes any additional accounting for dissolved material in retained liquid.
  9. Maya
    And the number alone does not say whether everyone will like the cup. We still have to describe what we taste.
  10. Noor
    Exactly. Nor is TDS a caffeine test. Keep strength, estimated extraction, sensory description, and customer preference separate.
  11. Maya
    Now suppose we add one hundred grams of water to all five hundred grams after brewing. Nothing else is added or lost in the example.
  12. Noor
    Then the total becomes six hundred grams. This is 5, not another extraction through the grounds.
  13. Maya
    The dissolved coffee material is still six point seven five grams. Dividing it by six hundred and multiplying by one hundred gives one point one two five percent.
  14. Noor
    Yes. The 6 falls, while the calculated mass of recovered coffee material stays the same.
  15. Maya
    Then six hundred times one point one two five, divided by thirty, still gives twenty-two point five percent for our beverage-based estimate.
  16. Noor
    Correct. A lower concentration after adding water does not mean we reversed the original extraction or removed coffee material.
  17. Maya
    Would the instrument necessarily display all three decimal places in one point one two five?
  18. Noor
    No. That is the arithmetic result, not a promise of instrument resolution or accuracy. Actual measurement needs the specified calibration, sample preparation, and reporting precision.
  19. Maya
    I will label the measured one point three five separately from the calculated diluted value, and keep both beverage weights with them.
  20. Noor
    Good. That makes the comparison reproducible without calling a calculation a new measurement or treating a preferred flavor as a universal target.

Answers and explanations
  1. beverage mass Turn 2. The specified beverage-based calculation uses 500 g collected coffee, not the 550 g water originally poured into the brewer.
  2. Total dissolved solids Turn 4. Total dissolved solids, or TDS, expresses beverage concentration here; it is not the percentage of the dry coffee dose recovered.
  3. dry dose Turn 6. The denominator for the specified extraction estimate is the 30 g dry coffee dose, not the water input or beverage mass.
  4. extraction yield Turn 8. The specified estimate is 500 x 1.35 / 30 = 22.5%; it is distinct from the 1.35% beverage concentration.
  5. dilution Turn 12. Dilution adds water after brewing, lowering concentration without extracting more material from the coffee grounds.
  6. concentration Turn 14. The calculated concentration falls to 1.125% because 6.75 g of dissolved coffee is spread through 600 g beverage.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Maya: The brew sheet has five hundred fifty grams of water and five hundred grams of coffee collected. Which figure belongs in this extraction calculation?
  2. Noor: Use the collected beverage mass: five hundred grams. The water input is a different measurement, and some liquid remains with the grounds and filter.
  3. Maya: The refractometer result is one point three five percent TDS. That means concentration, not one point three five percent of the grounds extracted?
  4. Noor: Correct. Total dissolved solids describes the coffee concentration in the beverage for this reading.
  5. Maya: So I multiply five hundred by zero point zero one three five. That gives six point seven five grams of dissolved coffee material in the collected drink.
  6. Noor: Yes, using the supplied measurement. Then divide that by the dry dose of thirty grams and multiply by one hundred.
  7. Maya: That gives twenty-two point five percent. I should call it the beverage-based estimate in this exercise, not a perfect measurement of everything removed from the grounds.
  8. Noor: Right. That practical extraction yield estimate excludes any additional accounting for dissolved material in retained liquid.
  9. Maya: And the number alone does not say whether everyone will like the cup. We still have to describe what we taste.
  10. Noor: Exactly. Nor is TDS a caffeine test. Keep strength, estimated extraction, sensory description, and customer preference separate.
  11. Maya: Now suppose we add one hundred grams of water to all five hundred grams after brewing. Nothing else is added or lost in the example.
  12. Noor: Then the total becomes six hundred grams. This is dilution, not another extraction through the grounds.
  13. Maya: The dissolved coffee material is still six point seven five grams. Dividing it by six hundred and multiplying by one hundred gives one point one two five percent.
  14. Noor: Yes. The concentration falls, while the calculated mass of recovered coffee material stays the same.
  15. Maya: Then six hundred times one point one two five, divided by thirty, still gives twenty-two point five percent for our beverage-based estimate.
  16. Noor: Correct. A lower concentration after adding water does not mean we reversed the original extraction or removed coffee material.
  17. Maya: Would the instrument necessarily display all three decimal places in one point one two five?
  18. Noor: No. That is the arithmetic result, not a promise of instrument resolution or accuracy. Actual measurement needs the specified calibration, sample preparation, and reporting precision.
  19. Maya: I will label the measured one point three five separately from the calculated diluted value, and keep both beverage weights with them.
  20. Noor: Good. That makes the comparison reproducible without calling a calculation a new measurement or treating a preferred flavor as a universal target.
Calculate the diluted concentration

For a simplified beverage-based calculation, a 25 g dose produces 400 g collected coffee at 1.25% TDS. Add 100 g water after brewing, assuming negligible added solids and no coffee-solids loss. EY% = beverage mass x TDS% / dry dose.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Barista: Dissolved coffee material in the original drink is ___ grams.

Answer and explanation

D. 5

Four hundred grams multiplied by 0.0125 gives 5 g of dissolved coffee material.

2. Lead: The beverage-based extraction estimate is ___ percent.

Answer and explanation

B. 20

Five grams divided by the 25 g dry dose, multiplied by 100, gives 20%.

3. Barista: The diluted beverage mass is ___ grams.

Answer and explanation

D. 500

The original 400 g plus 100 g added water gives 500 g total beverage.

4. Lead: The calculated diluted TDS is ___ percent.

Answer and explanation

C. 1.00

Five grams divided by 500 g, multiplied by 100, is 1.00%; the same recovered solids still imply 20% by the stated formula.

Check the answers, then read both exchanges aloud. Swap roles on the second reading.

Twelve cups, overlapping choices

Turn a group order into mutually exclusive drink counts while keeping coffee and milk choices attached to each cup.

Fictional advance office order: twelve small hot lattes, all without syrup. Four require decaf; three require oat drink; two require both. All remaining milk is dairy and remaining coffee non-decaf. No allergy has been reported. Named drink details are available. Requested pickup is 08:30; shift-lead capacity confirmation is pending.

Amina: Counter barista / Ben: Barista preparing the order list

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • oat drinks
  • overlap
  • modifiers
  • remaining seven
  • named list
  • requested pickup
  1. Amina
    The office wants twelve lattes. Four are decaf and three are oat. I initially added those to twelve, but that cannot be right.
  2. Ben
    Those are 1 within the order, not extra drinks. Do we know how many people want both decaf and oat?
  3. Amina
    Yes, two names have both choices. Every drink is small, hot, and without syrup. The other milk choice is dairy.
  4. Ben
    Then start with the 2: two decaf oat lattes. Put those two names in that group once each.
  5. Amina
    Four drinks are decaf in total. After the two with oat, that leaves two decaf drinks with dairy milk.
  6. Ben
    Correct. And of the three 3, two are already in the combined group. That leaves one oat latte with non-decaf coffee.
  7. Amina
    So far we have two decaf oat, two decaf dairy, and one non-decaf oat. That accounts for five cups.
  8. Ben
    The 4 are non-decaf dairy lattes. Two plus two plus one plus seven makes twelve.
  9. Amina
    Let me cross-check by coffee choice: four decaf and eight non-decaf. By milk: three oat and nine dairy.
  10. Ben
    Both checks agree. Keep the 5 beside those group counts so we do not swap somebody's choices while preparing labels.
  11. Amina
    Would it be enough to put oat on three cups and decaf on four cups independently?
  12. Ben
    Not unless the right two cups receive both. The totals could look correct while individual drinks are wrong. Check each label against its person's combined request.
  13. Amina
    No allergy is reported on this order. I will not label oat as allergy-safe or assume that it means the person cannot have dairy.
  14. Ben
    Right. If an allergy is disclosed, use the actual allergy process. Milk preference and a verified allergy arrangement are not interchangeable.
  15. Amina
    The organizer wants pickup at eight thirty. Can I send a message saying that is confirmed now that the numbers add up?
  16. Ben
    Not yet. That is the 6. The lead still needs to confirm capacity alongside the other orders.
  17. Amina
    I will send the complete twelve-drink breakdown to the lead, including the two decaf oat cups, and ask about that pickup time.
  18. Ben
    Good. Keep the common details on every group: small, hot, no syrup. Grouping the coffee and milk choices must not drop those instructions.
  19. Amina
    My final check is two decaf oat, two decaf dairy, one non-decaf oat, seven non-decaf dairy; twelve named drinks. Eight thirty remains a request.
  20. Ben
    That is ready for the capacity check. Once the arrangement is confirmed, communicate it to the organizer and keep any later changes attached to the same order.

Answers and explanations
  1. modifiers Turn 2. Modifiers change specified drinks within the twelve-cup order; they are not additional cups to add to the total.
  2. overlap Turn 4. The overlap is the two drinks that are both decaf and oat; counting them twice would distort the totals.
  3. oat drinks Turn 6. Three drinks use oat in total; two are decaf, leaving one non-decaf oat drink.
  4. remaining seven Turn 8. Twelve minus the five drinks in the other three distinct groups leaves seven non-decaf dairy lattes.
  5. named list Turn 10. The named list preserves the combination assigned to each person; correct overall totals alone do not guarantee the right drink for each recipient.
  6. requested pickup Turn 16. 08:30 is the requested time; the shift lead has not yet confirmed capacity, so the accurate drink count does not confirm the schedule.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Amina: The office wants twelve lattes. Four are decaf and three are oat. I initially added those to twelve, but that cannot be right.
  2. Ben: Those are modifiers within the order, not extra drinks. Do we know how many people want both decaf and oat?
  3. Amina: Yes, two names have both choices. Every drink is small, hot, and without syrup. The other milk choice is dairy.
  4. Ben: Then start with the overlap: two decaf oat lattes. Put those two names in that group once each.
  5. Amina: Four drinks are decaf in total. After the two with oat, that leaves two decaf drinks with dairy milk.
  6. Ben: Correct. And of the three oat drinks, two are already in the combined group. That leaves one oat latte with non-decaf coffee.
  7. Amina: So far we have two decaf oat, two decaf dairy, and one non-decaf oat. That accounts for five cups.
  8. Ben: The remaining seven are non-decaf dairy lattes. Two plus two plus one plus seven makes twelve.
  9. Amina: Let me cross-check by coffee choice: four decaf and eight non-decaf. By milk: three oat and nine dairy.
  10. Ben: Both checks agree. Keep the named list beside those group counts so we do not swap somebody's choices while preparing labels.
  11. Amina: Would it be enough to put oat on three cups and decaf on four cups independently?
  12. Ben: Not unless the right two cups receive both. The totals could look correct while individual drinks are wrong. Check each label against its person's combined request.
  13. Amina: No allergy is reported on this order. I will not label oat as allergy-safe or assume that it means the person cannot have dairy.
  14. Ben: Right. If an allergy is disclosed, use the actual allergy process. Milk preference and a verified allergy arrangement are not interchangeable.
  15. Amina: The organizer wants pickup at eight thirty. Can I send a message saying that is confirmed now that the numbers add up?
  16. Ben: Not yet. That is the requested pickup. The lead still needs to confirm capacity alongside the other orders.
  17. Amina: I will send the complete twelve-drink breakdown to the lead, including the two decaf oat cups, and ask about that pickup time.
  18. Ben: Good. Keep the common details on every group: small, hot, no syrup. Grouping the coffee and milk choices must not drop those instructions.
  19. Amina: My final check is two decaf oat, two decaf dairy, one non-decaf oat, seven non-decaf dairy; twelve named drinks. Eight thirty remains a request.
  20. Ben: That is ready for the capacity check. Once the arrangement is confirmed, communicate it to the organizer and keep any later changes attached to the same order.
Count each combination once

A ten-latte order includes five decaf drinks and four oat drinks. Three drinks are both decaf and oat. All others use non-decaf coffee or dairy milk as applicable. An 09:00 pickup has been requested but not confirmed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Counter: Decaf with dairy accounts for ___ cups.

Answer and explanation

B. two

Five decaf drinks minus three decaf oat drinks leaves two decaf dairy drinks.

2. Bar: Non-decaf with oat accounts for ___ cup.

Answer and explanation

A. one

Four oat drinks minus the three that are also decaf leaves one non-decaf oat drink.

3. Counter: Non-decaf with dairy accounts for ___ cups.

Answer and explanation

B. four

Ten minus three decaf oat, two decaf dairy, and one non-decaf oat leaves four cups.

4. Bar: The 09:00 pickup time remains ___ .

Answer and explanation

B. requested

Correct counts do not turn a requested pickup into a confirmed production commitment.

Check the answers, then read both exchanges aloud. Swap roles on the second reading.
